**How the backoff counts failures (#120).** `attempts` cannot be the count, because it counts permanent failures only, so a bookmark that fails transiently run after run would never back off. `enrichment_failures` therefore has a second count, `consecutive_failures`, which every failure increments, transient or permanent. The backoff window grows with it, and `attempts` still decides the Failed state on its own. No code clears the count on success: writing an enrichment deletes the failure record together with it, so the next failure starts a new record at 1. The retry action on `/held` deletes the record too, so a retried bookmark is tried in the next run, not at the end of its window. A Held bookmark is not an attempt, so it changes neither count. Raising `schema_version` starts a new record at the new version, so both counts start again. The column went into the migration that added the table (Phase 7), so the sweep (Phase 8) needs no migration of its own. Phase 8 decided the window's formula and its cap, below.

**Decisions (from the owner, 2026-10-25, #127).**

- **When a provider rejects Haystack's own account, the run stops.** That covers Jev or the LLM answering `401` or `403`, the LLM answering `402` (`billing_error`), Anthropic's `400` for a spend limit set by the organization or a workspace (#154), and GitHub answering `401`. At the first such answer, the sweep stops the run, records nothing against the bookmark (no attempt and no backoff), and logs the reason without any URL or title. The next scheduled run tries again, so enrichment resumes on its own once the key, the billing or the limit is fixed. A GitHub `403` stays `rate-limited`, which pauses GitHub for the rest of the run (see "GitHub enrichment").
- **Every 5th consecutive transient failure counts as one attempt.** With the attempt limit of 3, a bookmark that never succeeds, such as one whose LLM request always times out, reaches Failed after 15 tries and shows on `/held`. An outage shorter than about two and a half days, or about three at the hourly steady state (#133), can't fail a bookmark on its own, because the run stops early when its first calls all fail transiently, and a longer one can (see "What the outage rule doesn't prevent" below). _How it reads (confirmed by the owner, 2026-10-25):_ the count is `consecutive_failures`, which failures of both classes raise, so a transient failure that is the 5th, 10th, 15th… failure in a row, of either class, counts as one attempt. A count of transient failures alone would need a column of its own and a migration. So permanent failures in the run bring the count forward: after two permanent failures, the third transient failure in a row is the 5th failure, so it counts, and with the attempt limit of 3 the bookmark reaches Failed.
- **The backoff starts at 15 minutes and doubles, up to 6 hours.** A bookmark that has failed `n` times in a row waits `15 min × 2^(n−1)`, at most 6 hours, after its last attempt: 15 minutes, 30 minutes, then 1, 2 and 4 hours, then 6 hours. The windows between a never-succeeding bookmark's 15 tries add up to about 62 hours, so it reaches `/held` in about two and a half days. _At the hourly steady state (#133)_, each try waits for the first run after its window ends: 1, 1, 2, 3 and 5 hours after the first five failures, because a failure is recorded a few seconds into its run, and 7 hours after each later one. That adds up to 75 hours, so the bookmark reaches `/held` in about three days.

_Built 2026-10-25 (#127's first pull request, with #154, #155 and #157)._ How failures are classified and recorded. `enrichPending` itself is the second pull request.

- **The account class.** `FailureClass` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/failure-class.ts` is `transient`, `permanent` or `account`, and `statusFailureClass` gives `account` for `401`, `402` and `403`, for Jev and the LLM alike, so one status can't mean one thing for one paid service and another for the other. TypeSafe's [Request rules and errors](https://docs.typesafe.ai/api/errors.md), read on 2026-10-25, documents a `401` ("Missing or invalid API key. Check the `Authorization` header.") but no `402` or `403`, so for Jev those two have their HTTP meaning, which is the account's too. The Jev client doesn't retry an `account` failure, and `writeProseEnrichment` doesn't ask again. `BookmarkFailureClass`, `transient` or `permanent`, is what a failure record and a `failed` outcome can hold. The GitHub client keeps its own reasons, without classes, and reports a `401`, for the repository or the README, as `unauthorized`.
- **How the module reports it.** `enrichBookmark` throws `EnrichmentAccountError`, with the code `ENRICHMENT_ACCOUNT_REJECTED`, the step (`sensitivity`, `github`, `kind` or `llm`), the reason and the status, before it records anything. It is thrown rather than returned because the module already throws for what is the run's problem rather than the bookmark's, a configuration error or a D1 error, and resolves only to outcomes it has stored. A caller that stops its run for any thrown error, as "How the sweep calls it" below says it should, therefore handles a rejected account even if it doesn't know about it, while a new kind of outcome would be counted as whatever the caller does with outcomes it doesn't expect. The error's message and fields are fixed codes and a status, such as `A service rejected Haystack's account (llm:status:402)`, so it can be logged as it is. A GitHub `401` stops the module before the `kind` question, so the run pays for nothing more. When the rejection comes after the sensitivity answer, the noul stays stored, because it is an answer about the bookmark, not a failure.
- **The spend limit's `400` (#154).** The shared Messages request (`anthropic-messages.ts`) reads the first 4 KiB of the body of a `400`, and only of a `400` (`ERROR_BODY_READ_LIMIT_BYTES`, with `readText`, which moved from the GitHub client to `apps/worker/src/enrichment/bounded-read.ts` so both clients share it). Errors' example body is under 200 bytes, so that is many times what the error JSON needs, and a body that goes on past it is never taken for a spend limit. It classifies the body as `account`, with the reason `spend-limit`, when the body is Anthropic's error shape (`type: "error"`, with `error.type` and `error.message`), `error.type` is `invalid_request_error`, and `error.message` starts with `You have reached your specified API usage limits` or `You have reached your specified workspace API usage limits`. Those are the fields and the wording that [Rate limits](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/rate-limits) gives under "Setting your own spend limit", read on 2026-10-25: "When usage reaches a spend limit you set, requests return HTTP 400 with error type `invalid_request_error`. The message starts with `You have reached your specified API usage limits`, or `You have reached your specified workspace API usage limits` for a workspace limit, and states when access resumes." [Errors](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/errors), read the same day (`docs.anthropic.com/en/api/errors` redirects there), says "The API also returns a 400 when usage reaches an organization or workspace spend limit you set". [Workspaces](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/workspaces) says nothing about the response. **No page gives a stable field for it**: the tier's monthly cap has `error.details.error_code: enforced_spend_limit_reached`, but that is a `429`, which is transient anyway, and nothing documents a code for a limit Haystack set. So the documented start of the message, with the status and the error type, is the narrowest match that can be defended: a message that only contains the words, the words under another error type or status, the gateway's own `400` (`{"error":[{"code":2044,...}]}`) and a body that isn't JSON all stay permanent, and so does any other `400`. Errors also says to catch the SDK's typed classes "rather than string-matching error messages", but the SDK's class for a `400` is the same for every `400`, so it can't tell this one apart either. If Anthropic rewords the message, the `400` becomes permanent again, as it was before #154. A limit on the Claude Code workspace can answer `429` instead, which is transient. The result, and so the failure the module would store, holds no response text. Both adapters share the rule, because the gateway returns Anthropic's response, and the contract test covers it for both.
- **Every 5th transient failure in a row** is `TRANSIENT_FAILURES_PER_ATTEMPT` in `enrich-bookmark.ts`, applied in the failure record's upsert (see "Recording a failure" below): a transient failure that is the 5th, 10th, 15th… failure in a row, of either class, counts. A permanent failure that brings `consecutive_failures` to a multiple of 5 still adds 1 to `attempts`, not 2, because the rule is there for transient failures, which otherwise never count, and a permanent one counts already. A test runs a bookmark whose LLM request always times out 15 times, and checks that it is Pending after each of the first 14 and Failed after the 15th, through `deriveEnrichmentState` with the attempt limit of 3.
- **The window** is `backoffWindowMs(consecutiveFailures)` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/state.ts`, next to `deriveEnrichmentState`, with `BACKOFF_INITIAL_MS` and `BACKOFF_MAX_MS`, because it is derived from the failure record as the state is, and the sweep's selection only uses it. It is 0 for no failures, and throws a `RangeError` for a count that isn't a whole number from 0.

_Built 2026-10-25 (#127's second pull request)._ The sweep is `enrichPending` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/enrich-pending.ts`. Its caller is the scheduled run (#128, below).

- **What it takes.** The Drizzle `Db`, the `EnrichmentConfig` and, optionally, `SweepOptions`: `batchSize` (default `SWEEP_BATCH_SIZE`, 3), `concurrency` (default `SWEEP_CONCURRENCY`, 3), `attemptLimit` (default `DEFAULT_ATTEMPT_LIMIT`, 3) and the clients' `timing`. _Decided:_ the batch size and the attempt limit are parameters with defaults, because Phase 8 puts their configuration, a Flagship flag and a `var`, in #128, which reads them and passes them on. A batch size above `SWEEP_BATCH_SIZE_LIMIT` (3, Q3's ceiling), or any option that isn't a whole number from 1, throws a `RangeError` before anything is read, because it is a bug in the caller. #128's configuration module can use the limit as the flag's ceiling.
- **A run** refreshes `match_text`, selects the bookmarks, enriches them, and logs a summary, in that order. Its own D1 statements are the ones Q3 counts: the selection, and the refresh's read and its batch.
- **The selection** is `selectBookmarksToEnrich`, one statement, so Q3's count of the run's own statements is unchanged. A subquery picks the candidates' ids, oldest first by `created_at`, and reads at most one more than the batch size. _Decided:_ the bound is the batch size plus one, so a run reads the text of at most 4 bookmarks however large the backlog is, after a schema version change too, and the extra one says whether more are outstanding. A larger bound would only read text that the run doesn't use. So that the bound never fills with bookmarks the run would leave out, the subquery applies every condition: no enrichment at the current version, a failure record below the attempt limit, not Held, and outside the backoff window. Held has to be there too, because a Held bookmark stays Held until someone reviews it, and enough of them would otherwise take every place. The backoff is `backoffWindowSql`, a `CASE` with one value for each count below the cap, each taken from `backoffWindowMs`, so the two can't disagree, and `now` is a bound parameter. A test checks the two at every count from 0 to 8 and at 40, and that a bookmark is selected exactly when its window ends, at each count from 1 to 7. The outer query reads the candidates' rows, joined to their enrichments and failure record, as `readEnrichmentStateInputs` reads one user's, and passes them through `collectStateInputs` to `deriveEnrichmentState`, which still decides which are Pending or Outdated, so the sweep and `/held` can't derive the state differently. A test checks that the two agree for a bookmark in every state, at a batch of exactly the expected count and of one less, so a bookmark the SQL let through but the derivation dropped would take an expected one's place. _The scan's cost:_ `bookmarks` has no index on `created_at`, so the subquery reads every bookmark in every run, and sorts the candidates, which is about 288 × N rows read a day at every 5 minutes once the backlog is drained, for N bookmarks, and 24 × N at the hourly steady state (#133). 1,000 bookmarks make 288,000 a day at every 5 minutes, which is accepted at Haystack's scale, as a small part of what D1 Free allows. Q12, which confirms D1 Free's limit on rows read before Phase 11, counts it. An index on (`created_at`, `id`) would only help during a backfill, when the oldest outstanding bookmarks come first, because once the backlog is drained no row matches and every row is still read.
- **Concurrency.** Up to `concurrency` bookmarks are under way at once, because each of that many workers takes the next selected bookmark that hasn't started. At Q3's batch of 3, all 3 start together, which Q3 allows.
- **How a run stops.** _Decided:_ a rejected account, an outage and any other thrown error stop a run the same way. No bookmark that hasn't started is started. The run's `AbortController` is aborted, and from then on a bookmark under way sends nothing more and records no failure: the module checks the signal before each step's request and before it records a failure, and throws the signal's reason instead. An answer that has already come is still stored, the noul or the enrichment, because it was paid for and is about the bookmark, while a failure that arrives after the stop may be the same cause again rather than the bookmark's. Every bookmark under way is awaited before the summary is logged, so the summary counts what was stored, and no write is left running when the scheduled handler returns. A request already sent can't be called back. The Jev client takes the run's signal too (`askJev`'s optional `signal`, which the on-save path doesn't pass): once it is aborted, the client makes no other attempt at the question and stops waiting out a backoff, and returns the last attempt's failure, which the module then doesn't record. An attempt under way still runs to its answer or its own time limit, because the signal isn't given to `fetch`.
- **A rejected account stops the run the moment it is known.** `enrichBookmark` aborts the run's controller with the `EnrichmentAccountError` before it throws it, rather than leaving the sweep to abort when the error reaches it. A bookmark whose next step waits for a response would see the later stop in time anyway, but a GitHub read waiting its turn behind the pause (below) waits only for a promise, and a test showed it sent after another bookmark's GitHub `401` until this changed. GitHub's `401` is checked within the read's turn for the same reason.
- **The outage rule.** The run stops when the first `OUTAGE_STOP_CALLS` (2) bookmarks to finish, in the order they finish, all failed transiently. _Decided:_ 2, because one transient failure can be one bookmark's timeout or `529`, and the LLM's isn't retried, and because the number must stay below the batch size, or the stop could never spare a bookmark. At Q3's 3, it spares the third, which is under way when the second fails, so the third's own failure isn't recorded.
- **What the outage rule doesn't prevent, and why that is accepted.** In each run of an outage, the first 2 bookmarks still record a transient failure each. The backoff makes the next runs pick other bookmarks, and each bookmark is tried again when its window ends, so a bookmark tried at the end of every window reaches Failed after its 15th try, about 62 hours after its first, or 75 hours at the hourly steady state (#133). An outage longer than about two and a half days, or three hourly, can therefore move bookmarks to Failed, where `/held` shows them and their retry action brings them back. _Decided by the owner, 2026-10-25 (#127):_ this is accepted, because recording the failures that stop a run is what grows those bookmarks' backoff. Without it, two bookmarks that always time out would stay at the front of the queue and stop every run, so no other bookmark would ever be enriched.
- **The pause on GitHub** is `GitHubPause` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/github-pause.ts`, one for each run. A rate limit on either request turns it on: the repository's, which returns `ok: false` with `reason: "rate-limited"`, or the README's, which returns `ok: true` with `readmeFailure.reason === "rate-limited"`. From then on, a bookmark's read resolves to nothing without a request, and the bookmark is enriched from what it captured, as after any other GitHub failure. The pause lets one read through at a time, because a pause that took effect only when a bookmark's outcome came back would let the other bookmarks' requests through in the meantime. A read is at most 6 seconds, so that costs little of a run's 15 minutes.
- **The `match_text` refresh** is `refreshMatchText` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/match-text-refresh.ts`. It reads up to `MATCH_TEXT_REFRESH_LIMIT` (4) enrichments at a `match_text_version` below `MATCH_TEXT_VERSION`, oldest first, with their bookmark's title, and rewrites them with `deriveMatchText` in one D1 `batch()`, without calling any service. _Decided:_ only enrichments at the current `schema_version` are refreshed, because `deriveMatchText` reads `EnrichmentV1`, the shape that version stores. A bookmark whose enrichment is at another version is Outdated, so its next enrichment gets the current derivation. When a schema version 2 changes the shape, the refresh needs a derivation for each version it should still refresh. The stored kind and data are validated with Valibot when read, and a row that fails is left as it is and counted as `unreadable`. Only a change made by hand can cause one. It keeps its place at the front of the oldest-first order in every run, so 4 of them would stop the refresh for good. _Accepted (2026-10-25, #127's review):_ every run's summary counts them as `unreadable`, so the stop can't go unnoticed, and skipping past them would need a way to remember them that no other part of the sweep needs.
- **The summary** is logged once a run, as `Enrichment sweep:` with an object of counts: `selected`, `enriched`, `held`, `failed`, `unfinished` (selected, but not finished because the run stopped), `moreOutstanding` (whether more bookmarks outside their backoff window were outstanding than the batch held, so false once the backlog is drained), `refreshed`, `unreadable`, `githubPaused`, and `stopped`, which is `outage`, `account`, `error` or null. No URL, title or error message is in it. _Decided:_ it has no count of the bookmarks within their backoff window, and `moreOutstanding` isn't a count, because the bounded selection reads neither, and counting them would read every outstanding bookmark again, which bounding the selection avoids. Each waiting bookmark has its failure record, and the summaries of the runs that recorded them counted them. A thrown error is logged once, as `Enrichment sweep error:` with a plain object: an `EnrichmentAccountError`'s code, step, reason and status, or any other error's code or name. That is `errorCode`, which moved from `index.ts` to `apps/worker/src/error-code.ts` so both share it. A message is never logged, because one such as `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD_INVALID`'s holds the value. `enrichPending` resolves to the summary instead of throwing, so the scheduled handler can go on with its cleanup (#128).
- **Tests** are in `apps/worker/test/enrich-pending.test.ts`, with the module's side of `EnrichmentRun` in `enrich-bookmark.test.ts` and the pause's in `github-pause.test.ts`. Bookmarks under way at once send their requests in any order, so those tests answer each request by the bookmark it names (`byBookmark`). They cover every test in Phase 8's list that belongs to the sweep, #127's criteria from #118, #119, #122, #123 and #124, and Q3's worst case, in which 3 GitHub bookmarks whose Jev questions each succeed on the third attempt and whose first LLM output fails validation make exactly 30 fixture requests.

_Built 2026-10-26 (#128)._ The scheduled run is `runScheduled` in `apps/worker/src/scheduled.ts`, which the Worker's `scheduled()` handler in `apps/worker/src/index.ts` awaits. The runtime waits for the promise a scheduled handler returns, so it needs no `ctx.waitUntil` (see below).

- **The Cron Trigger** is the `triggers.crons` entry in `apps/worker/wrangler.jsonc`. It was `*/5 * * * *`, Q3's starting schedule, until #133 moved it to `0 * * * *`, at the start of every hour, once the backlog was drained (see "The on-save path" below). Go back to every 5 minutes while a backlog drains, such as after a schema version change, or while a `match_text` refresh is under way (Q3).
- **A run** reads the flags (see "Secrets and configuration") and `SWEEP_ATTEMPT_LIMIT`, runs `enrichPending` with the batch size and the attempt limit when `sweep-enabled` is on, and then deletes the expired sessions and the expired `auth_flows` rows, in that order.
- **The cleanup is independent of the enabled flag.** _Decided (#128):_ the plan said the disabled flag makes the handler do nothing, but the cleanup has nothing to do with enrichment, and turning enrichment off shouldn't keep old sessions in the database. So the flag stops only the sweep. The cleanup also runs when the sweep stops early, with a `stopped` summary, and when it throws. A run with the sweep off logs `Enrichment sweep skipped:` with the flag's key.
- **What counts as expired** is what the sign-in code already uses: a row whose `expires_at` is at or before the time of the cleanup, as `authenticateSession` and `consumeAuthFlow` stop accepting it. The two deletions are `deleteExpiredSessions` in `sessions.ts` and `deleteExpiredAuthFlows` in `auth-flows.ts`. `createAuthFlow` still deletes expired flows too, through the same function, because anyone can start a sign-in, so that keeps the table small however often sign-in is used, even if the scheduled runs stop. The run logs `Expired rows deleted:` with the two counts.
- **The attempt limit** is the `var` `SWEEP_ATTEMPT_LIMIT`, `"3"`, read by `readSweepAttemptLimit`, which accepts only a whole number from 1 written in decimal digits. _Decided (#128):_ any other value is a configuration error that stops the sweep, not a default. A `var` changes only through a reviewed deploy, so a wrong one is a mistake that shipped, and a guessed limit would change which bookmarks end up Failed without anyone choosing it, as a guessed threshold would change which are Held (`readSensitiveWarnThreshold`). The limit is read even while the sweep is off, so a wrong value is reported before anyone turns the sweep on. A flag is different: it changes live without review, and Flagship can be unavailable, so a flag falls back to its default.
- **A failed step fails the run.** When the attempt limit can't be read, or the cleanup throws, the run logs `Scheduled run error:` with the step and the error's code, finishes the other step, and then throws an error whose message holds only those steps and codes, such as `The scheduled run failed (sweep:SWEEP_ATTEMPT_LIMIT_INVALID)`. So the invocation's outcome is an exception, which the dashboard and the tail show, rather than an `ok` that hides it. The handler doesn't call `controller.noRetry()`, because neither Cloudflare's [scheduled handler](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/handlers/scheduled/) page nor its [Cron Triggers](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/configuration/cron-triggers/) page, read on 2026-10-26, says whether a failed scheduled run is retried. The first says the runtime waits for the handler's promise, so a single task needs no `waitUntil()`. A retry would only run the same steps again, which is safe, because the sweep is idempotent. The sweep's own errors don't fail the run: `enrichPending` logs them and resolves to a summary.
- **Tests** are in `apps/worker/test/scheduled.test.ts`. The harness triggers the handler with `scheduled()` and the defaults, which runs the sweep and the cleanup. The tests that need other flag values call `runScheduled` in the test's own process, with a fake binding and the harness's D1: the batch size flag reaches the sweep, a batch size of 4, 0 or 2.5 gives the sweep 3, a binding that throws gives the defaults, the off switch stops only the sweep, the cleanup runs after a stopped sweep, the attempt limit reaches the sweep, a wrong attempt limit fails the run after the cleanup, and a failed cleanup fails the run after the sweep. The cleanup's own test checks that rows expiring before and at the time are deleted and later ones are kept.
- **The fixture server** for local runs is `apps/worker/test/fixture-server.ts`, which isn't part of the Worker. It answers Jev, the Claude API, AI Gateway's Anthropic endpoint and GitHub on a loopback port, with answers built from each request that the clients' Valibot schemas accept, and it checks the headers each service needs. `apps/worker/test/fixture-server.test.ts` runs the sweep against it through both providers, so a client change that it no longer satisfies fails a test. Start it with `vp -C apps/worker run fixtures` (port 8789, or `--port`).

**Failed bookmarks are visible and retryable.** "Manual attention" needs somewhere to happen. The `/held` page becomes the review page for both Held and Failed bookmarks. Failed bookmarks show the last error and a retry action that deletes the failure record for the current version, which makes the bookmark Pending or Outdated again. The action is a POST with the usual session and `Origin` checks.

The fast path and the sweep can occasionally pick up the same bookmark at the same time. The `UNIQUE (bookmark_id, schema_version)` constraint and an insert that does nothing on conflict make that harmless. The only cost is a duplicate provider call. If one run's LLM call fails after the other run has enriched the bookmark, the failing run still records its failure, so a Current bookmark can have a failure record. That is harmless too, because the state checks for an enrichment at the current version before it looks at the failure record, and the next enrichment of the bookmark, at a later schema version, starts a new record (#126). Since #133, a test runs a sweep that enriches a bookmark while the on-save path for it waits for the LLM, and checks that one enrichment, the sweep's, and no failure record are left.

_Built 2026-10-25 (#126)._ The enrichment module is `enrichBookmark` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/enrich-bookmark.ts`. Its callers are the sweep (#127), which production has run since #130, and the on-save path (#133).

- **What it takes.** The Drizzle `Db`, the configuration (`EnrichmentConfig`, which `Env` has), the bookmark's row and, optionally, a time limit for each client (`EnrichmentTiming`), which the on-save path shortens. The row is `BookmarkToEnrich`, the columns the module reads, which the caller's own query selects. So the module reads no row, and Q3's 3 D1 statements per bookmark hold: the noul, then either the write's 2 statements or the failure record.
- **What it returns.** `enriched`, `held`, or `failed` with the step that failed (`sensitivity`, `kind` or `llm`), the failure's class (`transient` or `permanent`), its reason, its status when there was one, and the validation issue for invalid output. The class and the reason are the client's own, so the sweep can stop a run on an outage without classifying anything again. `enriched` and `failed` also carry `github`, GitHub's failure, when GitHub gave no data or no README, so the sweep can apply its rate-limit rule (see "GitHub enrichment"). A configuration error, a rejected account (`EnrichmentAccountError`, #127) and a D1 error are thrown, not returned, and none is recorded as a failure.
- **The steps** are the plan's, in order. The sensitivity gate runs only when `sensitivity_noul` is empty, and stores the noul with `sensitivity_checked_at`. A bookmark at or above `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD` without an acknowledgment is Held, and the module returns without asking GitHub, the `kind` question or the LLM, and without recording a failure. GitHub is asked when `matchGitHubRepository` matches the bookmark's `url`. The `kind` question and the LLM get the same captured evidence, with GitHub's when there is any. The canonical URL is in that evidence only as `describeUrlForJudgment`'s description (#157, below). The provider and the model come from `chooseEnrichmentProvider`, so `ENRICHMENT_PROVIDER` decides. The row's `provider` is that provider's `id`, and its `model` is the model that answered.
- **The Held rule has one implementation.** `isHeld`, in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/state.ts`, is what `deriveEnrichmentState` uses for the table's first row, and what the gate uses. `readSensitiveWarnThreshold`, in the same file, reads `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD` and accepts only a decimal from 0 to 1, such as `0.5`. `Number()` would read an empty value as 0 and `0x1` as 1, so a mistyped threshold could hold every bookmark or none.
- **The write** is one D1 `batch()`: the insert, which does nothing on a conflict with `UNIQUE (bookmark_id, schema_version)`, and then the deletion of the failure record at the current version. The deletion runs even when the insert did nothing, because the bookmark is Current either way. A test makes the deletion fail with a trigger, after the insert has run, and checks that neither change is left.
- **Recording a failure** is one upsert of the failure record at the current version. Every failure adds 1 to `consecutive_failures`, and sets `last_error` and `last_attempt_at`. A permanent one also adds 1 to `attempts`, and so does a transient one that brings `consecutive_failures` to a multiple of 5 (`TRANSIENT_FAILURES_PER_ATTEMPT`, #127), but a permanent one at such a multiple adds only 1. `consecutive_failures` counts both classes, so earlier permanent failures in the run count toward the multiple: after two permanent failures, the third transient failure in a row is the 5th failure and counts, so the bookmark reaches Failed. Four transient failures, a permanent one and a transient one leave `attempts` at 1, from the permanent one, and `consecutive_failures` at 6. SQLite evaluates every expression in an update against the row before it, so the upsert reads the count this failure brings `consecutive_failures` to as `consecutive_failures + 1`. A new record starts with `consecutive_failures` at 1, and `attempts` at 1 for a permanent failure or 0 for a transient one. A rejected account records nothing.
- **`last_error`'s format** is `<step>:<reason>`, then `:<status>` when the response had one, then `:<issue>` for output that failed validation: for example `sensitivity:timeout`, `kind:status:429` or `llm:invalid-output:use_cases.1: max_length`. Each part is a fixed code, a number or a `ValidationIssue`, which names only the schema's keys, list positions and Valibot's check names. A test answers each step with a failure whose body, or whose output's key, holds the bookmark's URL and captured text, and checks that `last_error` holds none of it.
- **Configuration errors aren't failures of the bookmark,** so they are thrown and never recorded: every bookmark would fail the same way, and recording them would move every bookmark the sweep tries toward Failed. They are all found before the first request: the module reads the threshold and the provider setting, then runs each client's own check of the keys and base URLs the bookmark could need. Those are Jev's (`assertJevConfig`), which every bookmark needs because the `kind` question is asked even when the noul is stored, GitHub's for a GitHub bookmark (`assertGitHubConfig`), and the provider's (`checkConfiguration()`, which `EnrichmentProvider` now has and both adapters implement with the same checks their `enrich()` runs). Without that, an empty Anthropic key would be found only after the Jev questions, and every run would pay for them again. The clients still run the same checks before their own requests. A test for each key, token, ID and base URL checks that nothing is sent or stored. _Changed 2026-10-25 (#155):_ a base URL's error was a plain `TypeError` without a code, and an empty, missing or unparsable one got `new URL`'s bare `TypeError: Invalid URL`. Every client now checks its base URL with `checkedBaseUrl` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/configuration.ts`, which throws `JEV_BASE_URL_INVALID`, `GITHUB_API_BASE_URL_INVALID`, `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL_INVALID` or `AI_GATEWAY_BASE_URL_INVALID`, the `<VARIABLE>_INVALID` form of the gateway's ID checks, for a value that is empty, missing or doesn't parse ("… is empty or isn't a URL") and for one that is neither https nor http on a loopback host ("… must be an https URL, or http on a loopback host"). The message names the variable, not its value. `configurationError` moved there from `anthropic-messages.ts`, so all four clients build their errors the same way.
- **No retries of its own.** The clients' and `writeProseEnrichment`'s are the only ones, and a test makes the worst case happen (3 sensitivity attempts, 2 GitHub requests, 3 `kind` attempts and 2 LLM calls) and counts exactly 10 requests.
- **The canonical URL is sent redacted (#157).** The gate judges `url`, never the stored `canonical_url`, so a canonical URL carrying a secret that `detectSensitiveUrl` doesn't recognize would have reached TypeSafe's `kind` question and the LLM provider in full. _Decided 2026-10-25 by the owner:_ the `kind` question and the LLM get the canonical URL only as `describeUrlForJudgment(url, undefined, false)`, never in full. That needs no other Jev question per bookmark, so Q3's per-bookmark count doesn't change, and the host and the word-like path stay as evidence. It gets no title, because the bookmark's titles are evidence of their own, and the flag stays false, because it is about a canonical URL that was dropped, which a stored one wasn't. A stored value that isn't an http or https URL, which `POST /save` can't store, is left out rather than thrown for. A test stores a canonical URL with a key in its path that `detectSensitiveUrl` passes, and checks that no Jev or LLM request holds it. The questions' wording didn't change: the description's placeholders, such as `<random:16>`, explain themselves, and Phase 9's review of the first enrichments is where to see whether the kind or the prose suffers.
- **The schema version** is `ENRICHMENT_SCHEMA_VERSION`, 1, in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/enrichment-schema.ts`, next to the schema it versions.
- **The attempt limit isn't part of this slice.** The module only counts attempts. The sweep (#127) and its configuration (#128) are what read the limit, so its `var` comes with them, as Phase 8 says.
- **How the sweep calls it (#127).** Its selection query selects the `BookmarkToEnrich` columns with the rows, and it calls `enrichBookmark` for each with the default timings, counting the outcomes for its summary. Its early stop reads the `failed` outcomes' `failure`. A thrown error, a configuration error, a rejected account (`EnrichmentAccountError`) or a D1 error, is a problem with the run rather than with the bookmark, so it should stop the run, not count as that bookmark's failure, and log the error's code, step, reason and status. _Built in #127's second pull request (below):_ the sweep passes `EnrichmentRun`, an optional fifth argument with the run's `AbortController` and its pause on GitHub, which the module reads GitHub through, so the module's own GitHub step is reused rather than copied.
- **How the on-save path calls it (#133).** `POST /save`'s insert returns the `BookmarkToEnrich` columns (`BOOKMARK_TO_ENRICH_COLUMNS`, which the sweep's selection uses too, so a column the module starts to read can't be left out of either), so the module reads no row. The on-save path calls the module inside `ctx.waitUntil` with a shorter `EnrichmentTiming` (see "The on-save path" below). Because #132 stores the save form's noul, the gate asks Jev the sensitivity question only when that check got no answer.
- **Tests** are in `apps/worker/test/enrich-bookmark.test.ts`. They run the module in the test's own process against the real local D1 from the test harness, with the threshold from `wrangler.jsonc`. Every outside request is answered by `apps/worker/test/enrichment-fixtures.ts`, which serves the Jev, GitHub and Anthropic fixtures' own handlers from one MSW server, because only one server can patch `fetch` at a time. Each fixture can still start a server of its own, as its client's tests use it (`apps/worker/test/mock-server.ts`). `seedBookmark` in `apps/worker/test/seed.ts` can now set the captured columns and the sensitivity columns the module reads.

The sweep also refreshes `match_text`. When the derivation changes, raise `match_text_version`, and the sweep rewrites `match_text` for enrichments at an older derivation version, directly from the stored `data`, without calling the LLM or Jev.

The sweep runs from the Worker's `scheduled()` handler on a Cron Trigger. The batch size and an enabled flag are Flagship flags, and the frequency is the Cron Trigger's schedule (see "Secrets and configuration"). Running inside the Worker keeps the D1 binding, the secrets, and the runtime identical to production, and small batches per run stay within the per-invocation limits in Q3. The first backfill is the sweep draining the backlog over a series of runs. A schema version change works the same way: raise `schema_version`, and the sweep re-enriches everything while search keeps using the older enrichments.

The sweep stays enabled permanently. The on-save fast path runs inside `ctx.waitUntil`, which has a limited time budget after the response is sent, and provider outages and rate limits happen. The sweep is what turns those failures into eventual success, without anyone having to notice. Once the backlog is empty, it runs less often, hourly since #133, and a run with nothing to do makes only the selection query, the refresh's read, the two cleanups and the two flag reads. The same handler deletes expired sessions and sign-in flows, whether the sweep is enabled or not (#128).

For controlled runs, `wrangler dev --test-scheduled` triggers the handler locally against the local database with fixtures. In production, the first real run uses the starting batch size of three from Q3, with the sweep enabled for a single run, and Schalk reviews those enrichments before the sweep drains the backlog.

_How to run it locally (#128)._ Start the fixture server, then `wrangler dev`, and trigger the handler once for each run with `curl "http://127.0.0.1:<port>/cdn-cgi/local/scheduled?cron=0+*+*+*+*&format=json"`, the route that Cloudflare's [Cron Triggers](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/configuration/cron-triggers/) page documents. Wrangler 4.151.0 serves it in every local dev session (it starts Miniflare with `unsafeTriggerHandlers: true`), so it needs no flag, and `format=json` answers with the run's `outcome`, with a `500` when the run threw. `--test-scheduled` adds an older route, `/__scheduled`, which answers "Ran scheduled event" whatever the outcome, so it isn't needed; without the flag, that path reaches the Worker, which answers `401`. Both were checked on 2026-10-26. Run `wrangler dev` directly, not through `varlock-wrangler`, so it gets no real key: set each secret to a fake value in an env file passed with `--env-file`, and point the four base URLs at the fixture server with `--var`, for example `--var JEV_BASE_URL:http://127.0.0.1:8789`. Wrangler gives values from its own environment priority over the env file, so start it with an environment cleared of any real key, such as `env -i PATH="$PATH" HOME="$HOME"`. Pass `--config wrangler.jsonc`, because after `vp build` the file `.wrangler/deploy/config.json` sends Wrangler to the build's output instead (see "Deploys"), and fails when that output is gone. Pass `--local` and the same `--persist-to` directory to `wrangler d1 migrations apply DB`, `wrangler d1 execute DB` (to seed bookmarks) and `wrangler dev`, so the run doesn't touch the usual local database. Flags can be set in the local store with `wrangler flagship flags create <app-id> <key> --local --persist-to <dir>`, which refuses to run while a dev session for the Worker is running. #128's pull request has a recorded run, which used `/__scheduled`.

**The on-save path.** `POST /save` calls the same module for the one new bookmark inside `ctx.waitUntil`, so new bookmarks are usually enriched within seconds. If the on-save path fails or is cut short, the sweep picks the bookmark up later.

_Built 2026-10-28 (#133)._ The on-save path is `enrichOnSave` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/enrich-on-save.ts`. It was deployed on 2026-10-28.

- **How it is called.** The Worker's `fetch` handler in `apps/worker/src/index.ts` takes the `ExecutionContext` (`ctx`) and passes it to the routes. `saveBookmark` takes an `afterSave` callback, which it calls once with the inserted row, only when the insert added a bookmark, just before it returns the "Saved" page. For `POST /save`, the callback hands `enrichOnSave(db, env, bookmark)` to `ctx.waitUntil`, so `save.ts` doesn't depend on the runtime's context. A link that is saved already starts nothing. The response doesn't wait for the enrichment. Only the module's synchronous start, its configuration checks and the building of its first request, runs before the handler returns, and everything after its first `await` runs while the response is sent or after it.
- **Its time limits** are `ON_SAVE_TIMING`, whose worst case for one bookmark is 27 of the 30 seconds that `ctx.waitUntil` allows (see "Jev client").
- **A failure leaves the bookmark to the sweep.** `enrichOnSave` never rejects, so nothing escapes `ctx.waitUntil` as an unhandled rejection. A failed outcome is recorded as the sweep records one, so the bookmark is Pending, and the sweep tries it again once its backoff window ends. At the hourly schedule that is the next run, or the one after for a bookmark that failed in the 15 minutes before a run. A thrown error, such as a configuration error, a rejected account or a D1 error, records nothing, so the next run selects the bookmark. A call that the runtime cuts short stores nothing more than it had stored, because the enrichment and the removal of its failure record are one batch, so the bookmark is Pending too. One failure on save can't move a bookmark to Failed: a transient one doesn't count as an attempt, and a permanent one is the first of the attempt limit's 3.
- **What it logs**, once per save: `On-save enrichment:` with the outcome as the module returns it and `elapsedMs`, or `On-save enrichment error:` with the fields the sweep logs for `Enrichment sweep error:` and `elapsedMs`. The outcome holds only codes: `outcome`, and for a failure `step`, `failure`, `reason`, `status`, `issue` and GitHub's failure. The error's fields come from `loggableError` in `enrich-bookmark.ts`, which the sweep now uses too, so the two can't log an error differently. No URL, title or message is logged. `elapsedMs` is measured with `Date.now()`, which "returns the time of the last I/O" and "does not advance during code execution" ([Security model](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/reference/security-model/), last updated 2026-10-18, read on 2026-10-28). So it measures the time spent waiting on the services and D1, which is what the 30 seconds are mostly spent on.
- **It has no switch of its own yet.** Until #199 ("Let the on-save enrichment path be switched off without a deploy") is done, switching `sweep-enabled` off doesn't stop the on-save path: the flag stops only the sweep (#128), and POST /save still enriches every new bookmark, so stopping that takes a deploy.
- **Tests** are in `apps/worker/test/save-enrichment.test.ts`, which sends `POST /save` to the Worker under the test harness, with every service answered by the enrichment fixtures. A test that needs the on-save path's outcome polls the Worker's logs for its last line, and never waits for a fixed time. The tests check that the new bookmark is enriched and its outcome logged without the address; that the "Saved" response arrives while the LLM fixture holds its answer, and the enrichment is stored once it is released; that the sensitivity question is asked once when the save form's check answered, and again when it got no answer; that a bookmark the gate finds might be private is Held, with no LLM request; that a link that is saved already starts nothing; that an LLM `529` records a transient failure, and the sweep's selection picks the bookmark exactly when its backoff window ends; that an LLM `401` is logged by its codes, records nothing, logs no other error, and leaves the bookmark for the sweep's next run; that a sweep run that enriches the bookmark while the on-save path waits for the LLM leaves one enrichment and no failure record; that the on-save path makes one attempt at a Jev question, where the sweep makes three; and that `ON_SAVE_TIMING`'s worst case is 27 seconds. The other tests that save have no LLM key, so their on-save path logs a configuration error, which `save-sensitivity.test.ts` leaves out of its checks of the sensitivity check's own logs.

Anthropic and OpenAI offer discounted asynchronous batch APIs, which would suit a large first backfill. At the expected collection size, the savings do not justify a provider-specific code path, so v1 does not use them.

## Search design

### Question design

Each query-bookmark pair is evaluated with this shape. Confirm the field names against the live API reference before implementing.

```json
{
  "model": "jev-1.13.0",
  "state": {
    "query": "<user query>",
    "bookmark": { "title": "…", "kind": "…", "match_text": "…" }
  },
  "questions": {
    "helps": {
      "type": "noul",
      "instructions": "Would `bookmark` help the person with what `query` describes?",
      "criteria": {
        "true": "The bookmark directly serves the need in the query.",
        "false": "The bookmark is only on a related topic, or unrelated."
      }
    },
    "relevance": {
      "type": "score",
      "instructions": "How well does `bookmark` serve the need described in `query`?",
      "criteria": [
        "Unrelated to the need",
        "Same general area, but would not help with this specific need",
        "Partially addresses the need, or is one of several reasonable options",
        "Directly addresses the need"
      ]
    }
  }
}
```

A Noul near 0.5 means that yes and no are about equally likely. It does not mean medium relevance. Do not treat the two signals as interchangeable when interpreting results.

`search_answers.score` is one number derived from a four-level distribution, and the plan does not yet say how. Before Phase 11, read the Score page in the live documentation. If Jev returns a single value, record how it is defined. If the client must derive one (for example, an expected value normalized to 0 to 1), write the formula here. The full distribution is stored in either case, so the derivation can change later through replay.

The query is capped at a fixed length in the shared search input schema (configuration, for example 500 characters). The cap applies to all channels. It matters most for Milestone 4, because agents can send long queries, and the query is repeated in every chunk's state.

### Jev model limits

These limits were provided by Schalk from the TypeSafe models page:

_Checked 2026-10-22 (#122) against the [models page](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models.md)._ The model, the price and both context limits are unchanged, and the page calls the second one the per-question context: the state plus any one question. Two things differ. The page no longer lists rate limits: they are set per organization, measured in tokens per second and requests per second, and shown in the [developer console](https://console.typesafe.ai/rate-limits), so Phase 11 should read the account's limits there before it sizes the limiter. And `jev-1.14.0` was released on 2026-10-14 as `jev-preview`, while `jev-latest` still points to `jev-1.13.0`. Haystack stays pinned to `jev-1.13.0`, as decided below.

| Property       | Value                                                                  |
| -------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Current model  | Jev 1.13 (`jev-1.13.0`)                                                |
| Price          | $0.042 per million input tokens                                        |
| Rate limits    | 250,000 tokens per second; 1,200 requests per minute                   |
| Context length | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for state plus the longest question |
| Input          | Text only: a string, a JSON object, or an array of text values         |

There are three consequences for the design.

First, **batch size has two budgets.** In batched mode, the state holds every bookmark in the chunk, so the state plus the single longest question must fit in 32k tokens. Everything in the request, including all questions, must fit in 64k tokens. Because every bookmark gets its own Noul and Score, the repeated question text (especially the four Score levels) grows with the number of bookmarks and competes with the state for the 64k budget. The chunker must compute both budgets from measured token counts rather than from a fixed bookmark count. Keep question wording compact, since it is paid for once per bookmark.

"Measured" needs a tokenizer, and Jev's tokenizer is not known to be available client-side. A tokenizer library in the Worker would also add bundle size and CPU time to every search. First check the live documentation for a token-counting endpoint, or for a statement about which tokenizer Jev uses. If neither exists, estimate tokens from character counts with a conservative ratio, calibrate the ratio in Phase 12 against `usage.input_tokens`, and keep a safety margin. If Jev still rejects a chunk as too large, split the chunk in half and retry it.

Second, **the requests-per-minute limit is the binding constraint for pair mode**, not cost or tokens (see Q1). The Jev client needs a client-side limiter in addition to backoff, so a burst of searches degrades gracefully instead of cascading into `429` responses.

Third, **pin the model version.** Use `jev-1.13.0` in configuration rather than `jev-latest`, so evaluation results remain comparable across runs. Model upgrades become an explicit change that is re-evaluated in Phase 12. The model reported in each response is still recorded on every `search_runs` row.

Text-only input confirms the existing design: voice is always transcribed to text before search.

### Request modes

**Batched mode** is the working default (see Q1). It places the query and a map of bookmarks keyed by ID in state, and asks one `helps` and one `relevance` question per bookmark. Each question references its bookmark by backticked path, for example `bookmarks.b_42`. Requests are chunked to fit both token budgets described above, and the chunks are issued concurrently.

**Pair mode** sends one request per bookmark. This matches the TypeSafe re-ranking cookbook and serves as the quality reference in Phase 12. Whether batching changes the probabilities compared with pair mode is unknown until Phase 12 measures it.

The mode is a configuration value recorded on each `search_runs` row.

### Ranking and thresholds

Thresholds are configuration, not code constants, and are stored on each run. The default ranking sorts by `score` and filters by `score >= score_threshold`. A parallel list computed from `noul >= noul_threshold` is logged but not shown. Which signal drives the displayed results is a Flagship flag, so the two can be compared in real use without a deploy (decision 20). Which questions are asked is also a flag. It defaults to both. After Phase 12 chooses a signal, it can be set to ask only that question, which gives back the batch capacity the second question uses. A flag change can take up to 30 seconds to reach every Cloudflare location. Every `search_runs` row therefore records the values the search actually used: the questions, the display signal, the thresholds, and the request mode.

If only one question is asked, the other column in `search_answers` has no value. Before Phase 11, decide whether `noul`, `score`, `score_probabilities`, and `score_confidence` become nullable, or whether single-question runs are logged differently. Replay must skip a signal that a run did not ask for.

**When Jev fails.** Search depends entirely on Jev, so a TypeSafe outage is a search outage. If some chunks fail after retries, show the results from the chunks that succeeded, state that the results may be incomplete, and log the run as `partial`. If all chunks fail, show an error state with a retry button and log the run as `failed`. Do not show an empty result list, because it looks like "nothing matches." A keyword fallback is in the backlog.

### Jev client

Write a small typed client with `fetch`. It sends the request, validates the response with Valibot, and retries with exponential backoff on `429` and `529`, as the API reference recommends. For the enrichment questions, it makes at most 3 attempts per question and gives each attempt a time limit, because Q3's subrequest count depends on it (#122). Phase 11 decides search's own retry budget. It also applies a client-side limiter below 1,200 requests per minute, and it estimates request size against both token budgets before sending (see "Jev model limits"). It surfaces `usage.input_tokens` for logging. The API key is only ever read server-side.

_Built 2026-10-22 (#122)_ in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/jev-client.ts`, for enrichment. The limiter and the token budgets are search concerns, so they come with Phase 11. Nothing in production calls the client yet. The fields, statuses and retry rules come from the [API reference](https://docs.typesafe.ai/api/system-one.md), [Request rules and errors](https://docs.typesafe.ai/api/errors.md), [Models](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models.md), and the JavaScript SDK's [RetryPolicy](https://docs.typesafe.ai/sdk/javascript/api/interfaces/RetryPolicy.md) and [TypeSafeClientConfig](https://docs.typesafe.ai/sdk/javascript/api/interfaces/TypeSafeClientConfig.md), all read on 2026-10-22.

- **The request** is `POST {JEV_BASE_URL}/v1/systemone` with `authorization: Bearer <TYPESAFE_API_KEY>` and a JSON body of `model`, `state` and `questions`. `questions` maps a name Haystack chooses to a question with `type`, `instructions` and `criteria`. A Noul's `criteria` has `true` and `false`, and a Choice's maps each option to its description. Each enrichment question is its own request, because the `kind` question may only be sent after the sensitivity answer, and it needs GitHub's data, which is fetched in between. That is the case the [primitives page](https://docs.typesafe.ai/primitives.md) gives for a second request.
- **The response fields Haystack reads** are `model`, the model that answered, which may differ from an alias that was asked for; `answers.<name>`, with `type` and, for a Noul, `noul`, or, for a Choice, `choice` and `probabilities`; and `usage.input_tokens`, a whole number. Fields Haystack doesn't use, such as `confidence` and `usage.output_tokens`, aren't checked, so a change to them can't fail an answer. The API reference makes `usage.input_tokens` a required integer, while the JavaScript SDK's `Usage` type allows `null`. The client follows the API reference, so a response without it is a permanent failure.
- **Attempts.** At most 3 requests per question: the first and 2 retries, which is also the TypeSafe SDKs' default (`maxRetries: 2`). Each attempt has 5 seconds, from the request to the last byte of the response, and a timed-out attempt is one of the 3. The SDKs default to 10 seconds. 5 seconds is still many times what the documentation's examples take (the [parallel questions cookbook](https://docs.typesafe.ai/cookbooks/parallel_questions.md) answers 13 questions over a 54,000-character article in 0.27 seconds), and it bounds the worst case for one question. Without a `Retry-After`, that is 3 attempts of 5 seconds and backoffs of 0.5 and 1 second, 16.5 seconds in all. With the longest `Retry-After` the client accepts, 5 seconds before each retry, it is 15 + 5 + 5 = 25 seconds. _Changed 2026-10-27 (#132):_ `JevTiming` also takes an `attemptLimit`, which is 3 when it is absent and is capped at 3, so a caller can make fewer attempts but never more than Q3 counts. A limit that isn't a whole number from 1, such as 0, 0.5 or `NaN`, throws a `RangeError` before anything is sent, because it is the caller's bug, and `NaN` would otherwise retry for good. The save form's check makes one attempt of 2 seconds (see "On the save form").
- **The on-save path doesn't fit those times, so it has its own (#133).** It asks both questions inside `ctx.waitUntil`, which has 30 seconds after the response (Q3), so with these times the two questions alone would take 33 seconds in the worst case without a `Retry-After` and 50 seconds with one, before GitHub and the LLM. The sweep itself is unaffected, because a Cron Trigger run has 15 minutes of wall time. _Decided 2026-10-28 (#133):_ the on-save path passes a shorter timing, `ON_SAVE_TIMING` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/enrich-on-save.ts`, so that its worst case fits the 30 seconds, rather than accepting being cut short. For one bookmark:

  | Step                                                               | On-save limit                                                           | Worst case |
  | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------- |
  | The sensitivity question, only when the save form's check got none | 1 attempt of 2.5 seconds                                                | 2.5 s      |
  | GitHub, for a GitHub bookmark only                                 | 2 requests of 3 seconds, GitHub's own                                   | 6 s        |
  | The `kind` question                                                | 1 attempt of 2.5 seconds                                                | 2.5 s      |
  | The LLM                                                            | 2 calls of 8 seconds, the second only after invalid or truncated output | 16 s       |
  | In all                                                             |                                                                         | 27 s       |

  That leaves 3 of the 30 seconds for the up to 3 D1 statements and the CPU time. One attempt means no backoff and no `Retry-After` wait, whatever the server asks for. Cloudflare's [Context (ctx)](https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/runtime-apis/context/) page (last updated 2026-10-12, read on 2026-10-28) says that `waitUntil()` "can extend execution for up to 30 seconds after the response is sent or the client disconnects", and that promises still unsettled then are cancelled, with a warning in Workers Logs. A cancelled call loses nothing, because the sweep enriches the bookmark later, but it logs no outcome, and an LLM answer that was paid for and arrives after the cut is thrown away. With limits that fit, the module always finishes: it stores an enrichment, records a failure or throws, and the on-save path logs which. A call that meets one of the shorter limits fails transiently, which records a failure without an attempt (see "Enrichment sweep"), and the sweep tries again with its own, longer limits once the backoff window ends. _The cost:_ an LLM answer that takes between 8 and 20 seconds would succeed in the sweep but times out on save, and the request it abandoned may still be paid for. #130's runs bound each LLM request at 4.4 seconds on Workers Paid, their wall time (see Q3), so 8 seconds is about twice that. #133's production check measured 5,390 ms and 7,375 ms for two real on-save enrichments, well inside these limits (see Phase 9). `apps/worker/test/save-enrichment.test.ts` checks that the on-save path makes one attempt at a Jev question, where the sweep makes three, and that `ON_SAVE_TIMING`'s worst case is 27 seconds.

- **What is retried.** Every transient failure, within the 3 attempts: a network error, a timeout, `408`, `429`, `529` and any other `5xx`. TypeSafe's errors page ([Request rules and errors](https://docs.typesafe.ai/api/errors.md)) recommends retrying `429` and `529`. The SDKs' default policy also retries `408`, `5xx`, connection errors and timeouts, which matches the transient list in "Enrichment sweep". `408` is a timeout, so it counts as transient, although "Enrichment sweep" calls every `4xx` but `429` permanent. Nothing permanent is retried: a redirect, any other `4xx`, or a `2xx` whose body isn't a valid answer to the question that was asked.
- **The backoff** is 500 ms before the second attempt and 1,000 ms before the third, each with up to a quarter taken off at random. Only these constants come from the SDKs (`backoffInitialMs: 500`, `backoffJitter: 0.25`), and 5 seconds, the longest wait below, is their `backoffMaxMs`.
- **A server's requested wait.** The [models page](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models.md) says the SDKs follow `retry-after`, and the SDK reference names `retry-after-ms` and `Retry-After`. The client waits for either when it is longer than the backoff, up to 5 seconds. What it does with a longer wait is Haystack's own choice, not the SDKs': the SDKs honour a wait up to `maxRetryAfterMs` (60 seconds) and use the backoff for a longer one, while this client stops the question. Retrying sooner than the server asked would most likely spend one of the 3 requests on another `429`, so the client leaves the bookmark to the sweep's own backoff instead. Only a whole number of seconds is read from `Retry-After`. A date, or any other value, is ignored, and the backoff applies.
- **The key** goes only in the `authorization` header, only to `JEV_BASE_URL`, and only over https, or http on a loopback host for tests. The client throws before sending anything when the key is empty (`TYPESAFE_API_KEY_EMPTY`) or the base URL is missing, doesn't parse, or is neither (`JEV_BASE_URL_INVALID`, #155), because those are configuration errors, not failures of a bookmark. The loopback exception is `isHttpsOrLoopbackUrl` from `@haystack/schemas`, the rule the GitHub client, the LLM adapters and Google sign-in use, so the integration tests can point the base URL at their plain-http server on `127.0.0.1` (Q10). _Changed 2026-10-25 (#126): the client first accepted https only, which would have refused that server before sending anything._ It doesn't follow redirects, so the key can't be sent on to another address. It logs nothing, and a failure is a fixed code (`timeout`, `network`, `status` or `invalid-response`) with the status, so a caller can log it.
- **A `401` or `403` is the account's failure** (#127). They were permanent, as "Enrichment sweep" said of every `4xx` but `429`, so a revoked or mistyped key would have counted against every bookmark the sweep tried, and moved each to Failed after 3 runs. Since 2026-10-25 they are the `account` class, which isn't retried, and the sweep stops its run for it (see "Enrichment sweep"). The errors page documents no `402`, which the shared rule makes the account's too.
- **Tests** answer every request from `apps/worker/test/jev-fixture.ts`, which uses MSW as the page fixture does and fails any request it didn't plan for. The responses are shaped like the documentation's recorded `jev-1.13.0` examples, because the tests make no request to TypeSafe.

## Design

The interface follows the "Night field" direction, which Schalk chose from three directions on a design canvas (#99). It is a dark interface with a straw accent, set in Space Grotesk, with addresses in JetBrains Mono, and the saved links are cards in a grid. The palette follows the system's color scheme: the dark palette is the default, and a light palette replaces it when the system asks for light.

**Where the CSS lives.** The styles are two plain files in `apps/web/public/assets`, served as static assets with no build step (#43). `tokens.css` holds the font faces and the design tokens, as custom properties on `:root`. `site.css` styles every page the Worker renders, and uses only those tokens. Every page links both, the tokens first, and `delete-dialog.js` gives the dialog it builds `site.css`'s classes. The file names are not hashed, which is fine, because the asset server sends `max-age=0, must-revalidate` with an ETag (see "Secrets and configuration"). The CSP gains exactly `style-src 'self'` and `font-src 'self'`, and nothing broader. Without `'unsafe-inline'`, no page can have a `style` attribute or a `<style>` block, and `apps/worker/test/pages.test.ts` checks the header and the markup for that.

**Tokens.** `color-scheme: light dark` is set on `:root`, and one `@media (prefers-color-scheme: light)` block swaps the colors, so a stylesheet never names a color itself. The colors, dark then light:

| Token                              | Dark                            | Light                           | Used for                                                    |
| ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------- | ------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
| `--color-ground`                   | `#121417`                       | `#F6F4EF`                       | the page background                                         |
| `--color-surface`                  | `#1C1F24`                       | `#FFFFFF`                       | cards, forms and the dialog                                 |
| `--color-raised`                   | `#23272D`                       | `#EFECE5`                       | secondary buttons and the notice box                        |
| `--color-border`                   | `#343A42`                       | `#D9D4C9`                       | the edges of cards and forms, which are not controls        |
| `--color-control-border`           | `#6B7178`                       | `#8A857B`                       | the edge of a field or a secondary button                   |
| `--color-field`                    | ground                          | surface                         | a field's background                                        |
| `--color-ink`                      | `#ECE8DF`                       | `#1A1C1F`                       | text                                                        |
| `--color-ink-secondary`            | `#CFCAC0`                       | `#3D3A34`                       | descriptions and the notice in the form                     |
| `--color-ink-muted`                | `#A9A49A`                       | `#5C5850`                       | dates, labels above the address, and the notice on the list |
| `--color-accent`                   | `#E9C46A` (straw)               | `#7A5A00` (ochre)               | links, addresses and the focus ring                         |
| `--color-accent-hover`             | `#F4DC9C`                       | `#5C4400`                       | a link under the pointer                                    |
| `--color-primary`                  | `#E9C46A`                       | `#E9C46A`                       | the primary button and the current navigation link          |
| `--color-primary-hover`            | `#F4DC9C`                       | `#F4DC9C`                       | the primary button under the pointer                        |
| `--color-primary-border`           | straw                           | `#7A5A00`                       | the primary button's edge                                   |
| `--color-on-primary`               | `#121417`                       | `#1A1C1F`                       | text on the primary button                                  |
| `--color-danger`                   | `#FF8A7A`                       | `#B42318`                       | Delete, the invalid field and the alert's icon              |
| `--color-danger-border`            | `#B5625A`                       | `#B42318`                       | the edge of a card's Delete button                          |
| `--color-on-danger`                | `#121417`                       | `#FFFFFF`                       | text on the dialog's Delete button                          |
| `--color-alert`, `-border`, `-ink` | `#3A2321`, `#6B3B35`, `#FFD2CB` | `#FDF0EE`, `#F0C2BB`, `#8A1A12` | the save form's error                                       |
| `--color-backdrop`                 | `rgb(8 9 11 / 72%)`             | `rgb(26 28 31 / 45%)`           | behind the open dialog                                      |

The other tokens are the type (body text at 17 px with a line height of 1.55, the `h1` at 40 px, weight 700 and letter spacing -0.03em, card titles at 19 px and weight 600, and the address form's title at 20 px and weight 700), a spacing scale from 4 to 64 px, the radii (10 px for fields, 14 px for cards, 16 px for forms and the dialog, and pills for buttons), the target sizes, the focus ring, and the two content widths. Sizes are in `rem`, so they follow the reader's font size.

The tokens are not registered with `@property`, which the "Search page" section asked for. A registered property needs an `initial-value` that does not depend on anything else, and a length in `rem` depends on the root font size, so only the colors could be registered. Registering only the colors would give the tokens file two styles for one job, so that is left for the search page to decide.

**Fonts.** Both families are self-hosted, because the CSP allows fonts from this origin only. They come from Fontsource 5.3.0 (`@fontsource-variable/space-grotesk` and `@fontsource-variable/jetbrains-mono`, published from `fontsource/font-files` on 2026-07-19, a project that releases regularly). Each is the Latin subset of the variable font, because the variable file is smaller than the static weights the design needs: 22 KB against 52 KB for Space Grotesk's four weights, and 40 KB against 43 KB for JetBrains Mono's two. Both are under the SIL Open Font License 1.1, and the license files sit next to the fonts in `apps/web/public/assets/fonts` and are served with them. The files were copied from the npm tarballs, and nothing depends on the packages, so Dependabot does not update them. To update a font, take three steps from the new tarball. Copy `files/<family>-latin-wght-normal.woff2` and `LICENSE` over the files in `apps/web/public/assets/fonts`. Copy the `unicode-range` of the `<family>-latin-wght-normal` face in the package's `index.css` into the matching `@font-face` in `tokens.css`, because a new release can change the subset. Then change the version in the comment at the top of `tokens.css`, and here. The faces use `font-display: swap`, the fallbacks are `system-ui, sans-serif` and `ui-monospace, monospace`, and the `unicode-range` is Fontsource's, so a character outside the subset falls back too.

**The popup shares the tokens file.** `apps/extension/src/popup.css` imports `apps/web/public/assets/tokens.css` by its relative path, and the extension's Vite build inlines it and bundles the fonts it names. So the palette and the type have one source, the Worker's assets keep having no build step, and only the extension's build, which already exists, reads the file. Copying the tokens into the popup would duplicate knowledge that must change together. Making `apps/web` a workspace package that exports the file would name the dependency in a `package.json`, but it would add a package and change how knip sees `apps/web/public`, whose entry is set in the Calavera-managed `knip.json`. The minifier drops the license comments in the CSS, so `apps/extension/build.ts` also copies the fonts' license files into the build's `licenses/` folder, and a build test checks that every bundled font has one. The build bundles both fonts, because `tokens.css` names both, but the popup uses only Space Grotesk. JetBrains Mono adds 40 KB to the extension's package and nothing else, because a browser never fetches a font face that no text uses.

**The header.** Every page has a header with the wordmark, which links to `/`. On a signed-in user's pages, it also has a `<nav aria-label="Main">` with "Saved links" (with `aria-current="page"` on that page) and a Sign out button. The button is in a `<form method="post" action="/auth/sign-out">`, so it works without JavaScript and passes the Origin check like any other form. Before this, nothing in the interface let a user sign out. The sign-in outcomes, the signed-out page, the not-found page for `/auth/*` routes (which never look up a session), and the address form and the validation page when they are shown before sign-in have the wordmark only. `htmlResponse` takes the signed-in state, the width and the current navigation link, so each page says which header it gets. The header adds three Tab stops before the content on a signed-in page, and one on a signed-out page. Its landmarks are what a screen reader user skips it by, and there is no skip link.

**Layout.** Form pages are 46rem wide, and the saved links page is 72rem, so its grid has room for columns. The header takes the width of its page's content, so the two line up. The saved links are an `<ol>` laid out with `grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fill, minmax(min(18rem, 100%), 1fr))`. Each card shows the date, the title (the `h2` link), the address, the description, and the Delete button pinned to the bottom. The date used to come after the description in the markup, so it moved to the start: a visual order that differed from the reading order would fail WCAG 1.3.2 and 2.4.3. Moving by heading still lands on each title, and the date is the line before it. The cards are grid items of the `<ol>`, and each card's own `display: flex` replaces `list-item`, so they draw no marker, and the list needs no `list-style: none`. The delete dialog is inside `<main>`, so it sets `margin: auto` to keep the browser's centering against the margins between main's children.

**The address form (#97).** On the saved links page, the address form comes before the heading, as the design shows it. It is a surface card (`.panel`), titled by its label at 20 px and weight 700, with the hint in muted text under it. The pill-shaped field, set in the monospace font, and the straw Continue button share a row capped at 46rem, so the field keeps a readable length on the wide page. The field grows from a basis of 18rem, so Continue moves under it when the row has no room for both, as it does at 320 px. The field has `min-inline-size: 0`, because a field's automatic minimum width grows with the text size and could otherwise push the row past the page. On its own page, the form's label is the page's `h1`, and the card holds the hint, the row and the sensitive URL notice. A rejected field gets the same danger border as the save form's fields. While the Worker looks the page up (#105), Continue's label reads "Processing…", in its usual style, because it is never disabled, and the status message "Looking up the page. This can take a few seconds." appears under the row, in the raised box that the saved links page uses for its own status messages. A lookup that fails says so in the same box. A rejected address gets the alert and the danger border that the server renders. The row and the status region share a wrapper, so the empty region adds no gap to the card.

**The review page (#129).** `/held` is wide, like the saved links page, and its cards are the saved links' cards in the same grid. Each kind has a section, "Held back" and "Not described", with an `h2` at 24 px and weight 700 and a short explanation in secondary ink, and a section with no cards isn't rendered. A card's top edge repeats its section's heading in color, 3 px of straw for a Held card and of the danger border for a Failed one, which is decoration, since the heading says it in words. A Held card's checkbox is drawn in `site.css`, because the browser's own box takes the system's accent color, and `accent-color` isn't newly available Baseline, which the style linter flags. The box is 24 px, WCAG 2.5.8's minimum, with a field's edge, and a ticked box has the primary button's straw and edge, so it keeps 3:1 on white. The tick is a border, so forced colors keep it. The checkbox has a row of its own, and Release, a small primary button, shares the row below with Delete: the release form has `display: contents`, which changes nothing about how it is sent. A Failed card's last error sits in the raised box of the site's messages, with "Last error:" in ink. The empty state is the saved links page's dashed card, with "Nothing to review." in ink and a link back to the saved links. The result of an action is in the status box, and doesn't take focus. The `.status > p:focus` ring is the address form's, whose script moves focus to its message. On the saved links page, the link to `/held` is a surface card with a 4 px straw edge at the start.

**The save form's warning (#132).** The warning that a link might be private sits in the form right after the notice about private links. It is the notice's raised box, in secondary ink at the small size, with a 4 px edge at the start, the mark that a Held card and the link to `/held` use for a link held back, and its first sentence is in ink and weight 600. The edge is `--color-primary-border`, straw in the dark scheme and ochre in the light one, because straw on the light raised box barely shows, and the edge is what tells the warning from the notice above it. The words say what it is, so the edge is decoration. The checkbox under it is `/held`'s box (`.checkbox`), with its label at the body size, in ink and weight 600, like a field's label, and the warning and the box share a wrapper with a 12 px gap, so they read as one group inside the form's larger gaps. A box the server rejected has the danger edge of a rejected field. The error is the form's usual alert after the heading. In forced colors, the raised background is dropped and the edge, the box's border and its tick, which are borders, are kept.

**Accessibility.** Every text color pair in the table meets 4.5:1 against the backgrounds it is used on, and each boundary that identifies a control meets 3:1 against what is next to it. The colors chosen for that are:

- The card's Delete outline is `#B5625A` in the dark scheme (3.82:1 on the surface), because the design's `#5A3A36` was 1.65:1. In the light scheme it is the danger color, `#B42318` (6.57:1 on white), because the design's `#D99A93` was 2.33:1.
- Fields and secondary buttons are outlined in `--color-control-border`: `#6B7178` is 3.74:1 on the dark ground and 3.35:1 on the dark surface, and `#8A857B` is 3.34:1 on the light ground and 3.67:1 on white.
- Straw on white is 1.67:1, so in the light scheme the primary button and the current navigation link have an ochre edge (`#7A5A00`, 6.38:1 on white and 5.81:1 on the ground).

Focus is a 3 px solid outline in the accent color, offset 3 px.
