The criteria describe a true answer as a link that works as a key (shares, signed links, invitations, sign-in and reset links, tokenized links) and a false answer as a link to a public page. Write the final wording after reading the Noul page in the live documentation.

_Worded 2026-10-22 (#122), after reading the [Noul page](https://docs.typesafe.ai/primitives/noul.md) and [Jev 1.13 jaggedness](https://docs.typesafe.ai/model-jaggedness/jev-1.13.md)._ The question is `SENSITIVITY_QUESTION` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/jev-questions.ts`. The state is `{ "link": <describeUrlForJudgment's output> }`, because the [State page](https://docs.typesafe.ai/concepts/state.md) recommends named fields, and the question can then point at `link` by path. Jev reads a question literally and has no other way to know what a placeholder means, so the instructions say it:

- **`question`:** "Would the web address that `link` describes give anyone who has it access to something private, such as a document, a file, an account or an invitation?" A high value means yes, as the Noul page asks.
- **`about_link`:** "`link` describes the address without its values. Each query value, each fragment value and each part of the address that does not read as words is replaced by a placeholder: `<empty>`, or `<class:length>`, where the class is digits, word, hex or random, and the length counts its characters. `link.title`, when present, is the page's title. `link.declares_sensitive_canonical` is present, and true, when the page named a canonical address that Haystack recognized as carrying a secret."
- **`criteria.true`:** "The address works as a key: whoever has it gets in without signing in, as with a shared document or file, a signed download link, an invitation, a sign-in or password reset link, or an address that carries an access token. `link.declares_sensitive_canonical` is a strong sign of this."
- **`criteria.false`:** "The address leads to a public page, such as an article, documentation, a product page, a search or a repository, which anyone can open without the address being shared with them."

The instructions and the criteria say the same thing from both sides, because the jaggedness page warns that Jev does worse when they disagree. The Noul page suggests trying a question with and without criteria. That comparison needs real bookmarks and their answers, so it belongs to Phase 9's review of the first enrichments.

**Policy, in code.** The deterministic match remains a hard refusal. A noul at or above `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD` (configuration) is a warning, not a refusal, because a probability will produce false positives. If Jev is unavailable, saving proceeds on the deterministic check alone, with `sensitivity_noul` left empty, and the enrichment gate checks the bookmark later. A TypeSafe outage must never block saving.

**On the save form.** `GET /save` runs the check before rendering. Above the threshold, the form shows a warning explaining why the link looks private and adds a required checkbox: "This link does not give access to anything private." `POST /save` runs the check again rather than trusting anything from the form, and rejects the submission if the result is above the threshold and the checkbox is not ticked. The stored bookmark records the noul, when it was checked, and the acknowledgment.

_Built 2026-10-27 (#132)._ The check is `checkSaveSensitivity` in `apps/worker/src/save-sensitivity.ts`, which `showSaveForm` and `saveBookmark` in `apps/worker/src/save.ts` call. It is the one place that calls `describeUrlForJudgment` for the form, and it takes the flag that the page declared a flagged canonical URL as a parameter. Both callers pass `declaredSensitiveCanonical(payload)`, #131's reader, on the payload after `withoutSensitiveCanonicalUrl`, so the flag is set when the save link or the form's hidden input carried it, or when that function dropped a flagged canonical URL just now. With the flag, the description has `"declares_sensitive_canonical": true`, and without it the key is absent. The canonical URL is never in the request, because the payload the check reads has already lost it. `POST /save` stores the same flag in `sensitive_canonical_dropped` (#131), so the form's check and the gate's judge a bookmark with the same evidence.

- **What Jev is sent.** The state is the gate's: `{ "link": describeUrlForJudgment(url, title, flag) }`, with `SENSITIVITY_QUESTION`. `url` is the address the form saves, without its tracking parameters, because that is the address the gate later describes from `bookmarks.url`, and the address that is stored and sent on. _Decided:_ `title` is the payload's `title`, the page's own document title, which `POST /save` stores as `captured_title`. It isn't the Open Graph title, and it isn't the title the user confirms on the form. The redaction's reasoning covers the page's own title ("evidence about the page and not a secret"), and the question tells Jev that `link.title` "is the page's title", but words the user typed into the field are neither. Judging the payload's `title` also means that `GET /save` and `POST /save` describe the link the same way whatever the user types, so `POST` can't answer differently from the warning the user saw because they edited the title, and a bookmark gets the same description from the form's check and the gate's.
- **Only a signed-in request that shows the form is checked.** A signed-out `GET` or `POST` goes to `signedOutSave` and sign-in before any route that checks, so it spends no Jev budget. `GET /save` checks after the duplicate check, so an already-saved link costs nothing. _Decided:_ `POST /save` now looks for an existing bookmark before it asks Jev, and answers with the "already saved" page at once, so a duplicate costs no request and its form never comes back asking for a box that would change nothing. The unique index still decides a concurrent submission, as before. _Changed in #132's review:_ a rejected title or description still gets the form first, before the lookup, as before #132, so a duplicate with a blank title gets "Check the form" (400) rather than "already saved". That form still asks Jev, because it shows the warning and the box, so the user isn't sent back a second time for the box after correcting the field.
- **The time limit.** _Decided:_ one attempt of 2 seconds (`SAVE_CHECK_TIMEOUT_MS`), from the request to the last byte of the answer, through the Jev client's new `attemptLimit` (see "Jev client"). The page waits for the check, and the address form's lookup can already have taken up to 3 seconds before it. The documentation's examples answer in a fraction of a second (the parallel questions cookbook answers 13 questions in 0.27 seconds), so 2 seconds is several times what a normal answer takes, while keeping the form usable when Jev is slow. A retry would add a backoff and a second wait to a page someone is waiting for, and nothing is lost without it, because the gate asks again later with the sweep's 3 attempts. A timeout is an outage.
- **An outage never blocks saving.** A timeout, a `429`, a `5xx`, a rejected key (`401`), an answer that isn't a noul, and a request that fails all give no answer. The form then shows no warning, and `POST /save` saves with `sensitivity_noul` and `sensitivity_checked_at` left empty, and the gate checks the bookmark later. _Decided:_ a configuration the check can't use, an empty `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`, a `JEV_BASE_URL` that isn't valid, or a `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD` that can't be read, is treated the same way, rather than failing the page with a 500. The sweep throws for the same error, so it is found there, and saving links doesn't depend on Jev, as the saved links page doesn't depend on the review count. Each case logs one line, `Sensitivity check skipped: <code>`, where the code is `timeout`, `status:<status>`, `invalid-response`, `network`, or the configuration error's code, such as `TYPESAFE_API_KEY_EMPTY`.
- **The warning** comes right after the notice about private links, and the checkbox right after the warning, inside the form (`sensitivityCheck` in `save.ts`). The warning is a paragraph, `#sensitivity-warning`: "**This link looks as if it might be private.** Haystack checked its address, and it looks like a link that lets in anyone who has it, such as a private share, an invitation, or a sign-in or download link. Save it only if it doesn't give access to anything private." Jev gives a probability and no reason, so the warning explains what kind of link it looks like, in the criteria's terms. It has no live-region role, because it is the form's content when the page loads, not an error. The checkbox is described by the warning (`aria-describedby="sensitivity-warning"`), so a screen reader user who tabs straight to the box hears the warning with it, and one who reads the form from the top hears the warning first.
- **The checkbox is /held's.** Its label, its field (`not_private=yes`), its markup and the server's reading of it moved from `held.ts` to `apps/worker/src/not-private-checkbox.ts`, which both forms use, so the confirmation is worded and sent the same way wherever the owner gives it. It is `required`, so the browser holds back a form without it, with or without JavaScript.
- **A submission without the box** above the threshold gets the form again (400), as a rejected title does: the page title "Check the form", the error "Tick “This link does not give access to anything private” to save this link." in the alert after the heading (`fieldError`), and the box with `aria-invalid="true"` and `aria-describedby="save-error sensitivity-warning"`, so the error is read first, then the warning. The values the user entered stay in the form. Focus is not moved, because the page is a server-rendered page that loads, and the title says the result. A browser reaches this page when the `GET`'s check found nothing or got no answer, so the form had no box, and the `POST`'s check warns. A form with the box reaches it only when the browser skips `required`, or by hand. Only one error is shown at a time, and a rejected title comes first, so a form with a rejected title and the warning shows the title's error with the warning, and keeps the box ticked if it was.
- **What is stored.** The noul and `sensitivity_checked_at`, the time the answer arrived, whenever the `POST`'s check got an answer. _Decided:_ `sensitivity_acknowledged_at` is set only when the `POST`'s own check warned and the box was ticked. A tick on a link that the check didn't warn about, or couldn't check, isn't stored. This follows the rule #175 set for Release: an acknowledgment always means that the owner was warned and said the link isn't private. Otherwise a form built by hand, or a tick from a warning that the `POST` couldn't confirm, could acknowledge a bookmark whose noul is empty, which the gate would then never hold.
- **A session that ends before Save** returns to the form with the title and description the user entered (#34), but not the box, because the form asks Jev again when it comes back, and the box answers that warning.
- **Tests.** `apps/worker/test/save-sensitivity.test.ts` runs the Worker with the Jev fixture: the warning and the checkbox at and above the threshold and neither below it, with valid markup; the request Jev gets, which is the gate's description of the saved address with the payload's title, and with `declares_sensitive_canonical` only when the flag was recorded, in each way it can be (see Phase 9's note); each kind of outage, with one attempt each, on `GET` and on `POST`; a late answer, which `checkSaveSensitivity` is tested for in the test's own process with a 100 ms limit, through its `timing` parameter, rather than wait out the real 2 seconds; no request for a signed-out `GET` or `POST`, an already-saved link or the address form; the rejected submission after a `GET` that showed no box; what is stored with and without the box, above and below the threshold; a rejected title with the warning; the return through sign-in; and both configuration errors. `apps/worker/e2e/save-sensitivity.e2e.ts` covers the warning, the browser holding back an unticked box, the rejected submission and its focus, and an outage, with and without JavaScript. `apps/worker/e2e/save-sensitivity.voiceover.ts` checks that VoiceOver reads the warning before the checkbox, and as the checkbox's description. It was written without a local run, so its first CI run is its first check. The design tests are under "Testing strategy", and the journeys are in `docs/journeys/save-form.feature` and `errors.feature`.

**As an enrichment gate.** The enrichment module runs the check as its first step for any bookmark whose `sensitivity_noul` is empty, which covers everything saved in Milestone 1 and anything saved during a Jev outage. A bookmark at or above the threshold without an acknowledgment becomes Held: it is not sent to GitHub, to the `kind` question, or to the LLM provider. A Held bookmark does not count as a failed attempt.

_Built 2026-10-25 (#126)_, in `enrichBookmark` (see "Enrichment sweep"). The title the gate gives `describeUrlForJudgment` is `captured_title`, the page's own title, and none when the bookmark has none. The redaction is written for "the captured page title", which is evidence about the page and not a secret, and the question tells Jev that `link.title` "is the page's title". `title`, the one the user confirmed, can be the page's Open Graph title or words of the user's own, which the redaction's reasoning doesn't cover. The save form's check (#132) judges the payload's `title`, which is what `captured_title` stores, so a bookmark gets the same description from either check (see "On the save form" above). The flag comes from `sensitive_canonical_dropped`, and a `NULL` counts as not set. `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD` is `0.5`, the value Schalk chose.

**Reviewing Held bookmarks.** A page at `/held` lists them with their titles and hosts. The same page also lists Failed bookmarks with a retry action (see "Enrichment sweep"). Each Held bookmark has two actions: release (the user confirms the link is not private, which sets the acknowledgment and lets the sweep enrich it) or delete (which removes the bookmark and everything attached to it). Both are POST actions with the usual session and `Origin` checks. Deletion here uses the same endpoint as everywhere else (see "Deleting bookmarks").

_Built 2026-10-27 (#129)._ The page and its two actions are in `apps/worker/src/held.ts`, and the routes are `GET /held`, `POST /bookmarks/:id/release` and `POST /bookmarks/:id/retry`, next to the delete route in `apps/worker/src/bookmark-paths.ts`.

- **One derivation, in one query.** The page passes each of the user's bookmarks through `collectStateInputs` and `deriveEnrichmentState`, with the policy the sweep uses: `ENRICHMENT_SCHEMA_VERSION`, `readSensitiveWarnThreshold` and `readSweepAttemptLimit` (`readReviewPolicy`). _Decided (#129's review):_ the rows come from `selectStateInputRows` in `apps/worker/src/enrichment/state-inputs.ts`, which `readEnrichmentStateInputs` now calls too. It has the state input columns and the joins, and takes extra columns, which `collectStateInputs` keeps, as the sweep's selection keeps its own. So `/held` reads the title, the URL, the date and the failure record's `last_error` in the same query, rather than reading the user's bookmarks a second time, and the page, search and the saved links page's count can't build the inputs differently. A bookmark that is Held and at the attempt limit is Held only, as the table's order says. Both lists are newest first, as the saved links are.
- **What a card shows.** The date saved, the title as an `h3` (the page's sections have the `h2`s), and the host, not the address, because the rest of a Held link's address may be what makes it private. The title isn't a link, for the same reason. A Failed card adds "Last error:" and a sentence built from the two fixed codes that `last_error` starts with, the step and the reason, for example "Writing its description failed, because the service took too long to answer." (`failureInPlainWords`). The page never shows the stored text. A record whose codes aren't in the lists, which only a change made by hand can store, reads "Haystack couldn't describe this link."
- **Release** is a form on the Held card, with the required checkbox "This link does not give access to anything private", the save form's wording, and a Release button. _Decided:_ the checkbox is the confirmation, rather than a confirmation page or a dialog, because it works the same with and without JavaScript, and the browser asks for it before the form is sent. The server checks it again (`not_private=yes`), and without it answers 400 with the page and "“title” was not released. Tick “This link does not give access to anything private” to release it." A release sets `sensitivity_acknowledged_at` to the time, which is what "Calibration" below reads. _Decided (CodeRabbit's review of #175):_ only a bookmark that is Held is released, so an acknowledgment always means that the owner saw the link held back and released it. Without that check, a request built by hand could acknowledge a bookmark whose noul is still NULL, which would then skip the gate when Jev checks it, and which a lowered threshold wouldn't hold. The bookmark's state is derived first (`readOwnBookmark`, through `selectStateInputRows` with its ID). A bookmark acknowledged already gets the same answer as a first release, without a write, so a reload or a second tab is harmless and the first time is kept. Any other state writes nothing and answers 409 with `/held` and "“title” isn't held back, so there was nothing to release.", because the request is well formed and the bookmark exists, but it conflicts with the bookmark's state, which happens when the threshold changed after the page loaded. The check and the write are two statements, which doesn't matter: the sweep never acknowledges a bookmark and writes a noul only when there is none, which a Held bookmark has, and the threshold changes only with a deploy, so the write doesn't repeat `isHeld` in SQL. A Held bookmark that had already reached the attempt limit is Failed once released, because Failed comes after Held in the table, so the sweep leaves it alone. _Decided (#129's review):_ the page's own rows, read after the release, say which state the bookmark is in, and for a Failed one the message is "Released “title”. Haystack couldn't describe it before, so it's now under “Not described”, where you can retry it." instead of "Released “title”. Haystack can now describe it."
- **Retry** deletes the failure record at `ENRICHMENT_SCHEMA_VERSION` only. A record at another version is that version's count, so it stays. The record also holds the backoff window, so the bookmark is selected in the next run. _Decided (CodeRabbit's review of #175):_ only a Failed bookmark is retried, so a request built by hand can't reset the backoff of a bookmark the sweep is still trying and move it ahead of the others. The state is derived first, as for Release. A bookmark with no record at the current version, as after a reload, gets the same answer as a retry. One with a record that isn't Failed, below the attempt limit or Held, writes nothing and answers 409 with "“title” isn't waiting for a retry." The deletion checks the owner itself, with an `exists` subquery on `bookmarks`, and that the record's `attempts` is at the attempt limit, which is the part of Failed the record holds, so it can't touch another user's record or one below the limit whatever happens between the two statements. In between, the sweep can only make a Failed bookmark more Failed, because it doesn't select one, and it can't make a bookmark Held.
- **Delete** posts to the delete endpoint with `return_to=/held`, and the delete answers with `/held` (see "Deleting bookmarks").
- **The policy is read before anything is written.** `showHeld` takes the policy rather than the configuration, so Release, Retry and a delete that returns to `/held` read it first. _Decided (CodeRabbit's review of #175):_ a configuration that can't be read then answers 500 with nothing changed, rather than after the bookmark was deleted or released. A delete that returns to the saved links doesn't need the policy, and is unchanged.
- **The result of each action** is shown as the saved links page shows a deletion: the page's title starts with it ("Released “title”", "Retrying “title”", "Deleted “title”"), and the status message says it. _Decided:_ Release and Retry are ordinary forms that load the page again, with or without JavaScript, because each is one quick write, so there is no script to send them with `fetch`. Focus is not moved when the page loads, as on the saved links page after a deletion without JavaScript: the page starts from the top, and its title says what happened, so a screen reader announces the result through the title. (#175 first gave the message `tabindex="-1"` and `autofocus`; #181 took them out, because a page that loads leaves focus to the browser, with or without JavaScript, as every other result page does, and only the script-driven delete dialog moves focus.)
- **Ownership.** Every lookup matches the ID and the user together, and a miss gets the delete's 404 page, which moved to `apps/worker/src/pages.ts` as `notSavedPage`.
- **Without a session,** a form sent from `/held` (Release, Retry, or a Delete with `return_to=/held`) goes through sign-in and back to `/held`, and a script still gets the 401.
- **The way to the page.** The saved links page shows "Review N links that Haystack held back or couldn't describe." under its heading, linking to `/held`, only when N is more than 0. The count comes from the same derivation, through `readEnrichmentStateInputs`. _Decided:_ when the threshold or the attempt limit can't be read, `/held` answers 500, because it can't tell which bookmarks to show, but the saved links page logs `Review count skipped:` with the error's code and goes on without the link, because saving and listing links don't need the state. Each view of the saved links page reads every one of the user's bookmarks once more for the count, which is accepted at Haystack's scale.
- **Tests** are in `apps/worker/test/held.test.ts` (the page, both actions, deleting from `/held`, the link, and the misconfigured case), `apps/worker/e2e/held.e2e.ts` (each action with and without JavaScript), `design.e2e.ts` (axe in both schemes, 320 px, and the checkbox in forced colors) and `apps/worker/e2e/held.voiceover.ts` (the announcement of Release and of Retry). The browser tests make a saved link Held or Failed with the `run` fixture (`e2e/review-items.ts`), because only the sweep sets those rows. The journeys are `docs/journeys/held.feature`.

**Calibration.** Stored bookmarks keep their noul and acknowledgment, which shows how often the warning fired on links the user considered fine. When a warning is shown but the bookmark is never saved, log only the noul and the fact that a warning was shown, never the URL.

_Built 2026-10-27 (#132)._ The Worker can't know, when it shows the warning, whether a save will follow, so every page that shows the warning logs one line at `info`, `Sensitivity warning shown:` with `{ noul }` and nothing else (`logWarningShown` in `save-sensitivity.ts`). That is the `GET` that shows the form, and each `POST` that shows the form again. A ticked submission that saves doesn't log, because the warning isn't shown again, and the bookmark's noul and `sensitivity_acknowledged_at` record it. So the warnings shown are the log lines, and the warnings that ended in a save are the bookmarks with an acknowledgment. A form that comes back after a rejected title logs the warning again, so the log counts pages shown, not links. The tests check that the log holds the noul and no part of the address or the titles.

## Deleting bookmarks

A bookmark can be deleted by its owner from the recent saves list (Milestone 1), the search results (Milestone 3), and the review page at `/held` (Milestone 2). All three use one endpoint, `POST /bookmarks/:id/delete`, with the usual session and `Origin` checks.

The server looks the bookmark up by ID _and_ the session's user ID in the same query. If there is no match, it returns 404, whether the bookmark does not exist or belongs to someone else, so the response never reveals that another user's bookmark exists. The delete button is only rendered for bookmarks the current user owns; in v1 every bookmark a user can see is their own, but the server check is what enforces it.

Deletion is permanent and removes the bookmark with its enrichments, failure records, and search answers. The URL can be saved again afterward. Search runs themselves are kept, so the evaluation harness must tolerate labeled bookmark IDs that no longer exist.

**Interaction.** Each result card and list item has a real `<button>` inside a small `<form method="post">`, with an accessible name that includes the title (for example, "Delete Vitest browser mode guide"), so a screen reader user hearing a list of delete buttons knows which is which. Without JavaScript, the button leads to a confirmation page with a single confirm button. With JavaScript, confirmation happens in a native `<dialog>` opened with `showModal()`, which makes the rest of the page inert and handles Escape. It does not trap Tab: from the first or last control, Tab and Shift+Tab can move to the browser's own toolbar, and the next Tab returns to the dialog. After deletion, the item is removed, focus moves to the next item (the previous one when the bottom item was deleted, or the list heading when the list is empty), and the live region announces the deletion.

Deleting without JavaScript was implemented in #42:

- Each list item's form posts to `POST /bookmarks/:id/delete`. Without `confirm=yes` in the body, the endpoint only shows the confirmation page, which posts again with it. A body that is not a form is treated as unconfirmed. The JavaScript dialog (#43) will post `confirm=yes` directly.
- The button is `aria-labelledby` itself and the item's `<h2>`, so its name is "Delete" followed by the title, and the visible text stays "Delete".
- Confirming shows the list (200, no redirect) with `<p role="status">Deleted “title”.</p>`, and the page title starts with the same words, because a screen reader announces the title on load but not a status message that arrives with the page. Reloading posts again and gets the 404, because the bookmark is gone.
- Without a session, a Delete button's form submission, on the list or on the confirmation page, goes through sign-in and returns to the saved links, and nothing is deleted (#34, see "Sessions that end before a form is sent"). The dialog's `fetch` still gets the 401.
- Every miss (no such bookmark, already deleted, or another user's) gets the same 404 page, "This link isn't in your saved links", which links to the list.
- Without JavaScript, focus starts at the top of the page after deleting. Moving focus to the next item is the dialog's job (#43).
- The confirmation page links back to `/#bookmark-<id>` with "Keep it and go back to your saved links".
- Deleting a bookmark deletes its enrichments and failure records, because `enrichments` and `enrichment_failures` reference it with `ON DELETE CASCADE` (#120). The only table still to come that references `bookmarks` is `search_answers`, in Milestone 3, and it needs `ON DELETE CASCADE` on its bookmark reference too.

The dialog was implemented in #43:

- `apps/web/public/assets/delete-dialog.js` is a module script that the list page loads. The CSP gains `script-src 'self'` and `connect-src 'self'`, and no inline script.
- The script builds the dialog with `textContent`, so no markup is parsed in the browser. The dialog itself has initial focus, so a screen reader announces it before any button, and a second Enter neither deletes nor cancels. Its accessible name is the heading plus the link's title ("Delete this link? A guide"), and its description is the permanence notice. The title is in the name because VoiceOver, in trial runs on macOS 26.6.2 with Playwright's WebKit, read the dialog's name and its heading when it took focus, but not its description (#56). With the title in the name, three further trial runs announced "Delete this link? [title] web dialog with 5 items heading level 2 Delete this link?". The visible heading stays short. Cancel comes before Delete, so it is the first button Tab reaches, and the button `showModal` focuses if focusing the dialog fails. The script focuses the dialog, which has `tabindex="-1"`, because Chromium ignores `autofocus` on the dialog element itself.
- Confirming posts `confirm=yes` with `fetch` and uses only the status: 200 removes the item and announces "Deleted …", 404 removes it and announces that it was already deleted, 401 says the user is signed out, and anything else announces that the delete failed and keeps the item. While the request runs, Cancel and Escape do nothing and no other dialog opens, so closing the dialog never looks as if it stopped a delete the server goes on to make. The buttons use `aria-disabled`, so focus stays in the dialog (VoiceOver reads the Delete button as "dimmed" while the request runs). The script closes the dialog, moves focus, and writes the message to the live region 100 ms later, because VoiceOver does not speak a message that is written in the same task as the dialog closing (#56). Three orders were measured on the `macos-26` runner with the repeated sampling described under "Screen reader tests": closing the dialog, writing the message and then moving focus 2.5 s later (run 38944180179), and closing, moving focus and writing the message in the same task (run 38946495256, twice), never made "Deleted …" VoiceOver's last phrase, while writing it 100 ms after focus moved did (run 38944739013). The first guess, that a screen reader ignores changes to the page behind a modal dialog because it is inert, does not explain it alone, because writing the message after the dialog closed and focus had moved, in the same task, still failed. 100 ms was the delay that was tested then, not a measured minimum, and the cause inside VoiceOver or WebKit is not known. It stopped being enough in #105, so the delay is now 500 ms (see the next paragraph). A minimal page with a button that opens a modal dialog, a button in the dialog that closes it, and a `role="status"` paragraph outside it shows the same thing: text written in the same task as `dialog.close()` and a focus move is not spoken, and the same text written 100 ms later is (run 38950012421). So this looks like a VoiceOver or WebKit problem and not a Haystack one, and it is to be reported to Apple after a check in Safari. A Chromium test checks what the script controls: when the message is written, the dialog is closed, focus is on the next item's heading (after a failure, on the Delete button), and at least 490 ms have passed since `dialog.close()`. The response is the full list page, which costs one list query. A smaller answer for scripts is not worth a second response format.
- **The delay became 500 ms in #105.** The address form gave the saved links page a second `role="status"` region, empty, in the form above the list heading. With it, the VoiceOver test for confirming a delete failed every time with the 100 ms delay: VoiceOver read the heading that took focus ("heading level 1 Saved links main"), then its hint, and never the message, although the message was in the list's live region (0 of 13 tries, in runs 39399981976, 39415866900 and 39415947993). Guidepup's debug log, which samples VoiceOver's last phrase every 50 ms, showed the message was never spoken, so the test did not just miss it. The same page without the address form's region passed 7 of 12 tries (runs 39415922108 and 39417460255), and with the region but without the address form's script, 0 of 6 (run 39417456632), so the empty region, not the script, made it worse. Writing the message 500 ms after focus moved was spoken in 6 of 6 tries with the region on the page (run 39417453189), and 1500 ms in 5 of 5 (run 39417449494, whose sixth try failed because VoiceOver did not start). With 500 ms, VoiceOver starts reading the heading and then reads "Deleted …"; with 1500 ms it finishes the heading first. 500 ms is the shorter of the two delays that worked. With the change committed, the test passed 8 of 8 tries (run 39418952111), and every other VoiceOver test except the address form's passed once (run 39418955671). `main`, which has no second region, already failed this test about half the time with 100 ms (#102), which suggests the delay was always close to VoiceOver's limit. A second Delete opened within those 500 ms loses the first message, because the page is inert again before it is written. That is accepted, because the user would have to move to another Delete button and press it in that time. The address form's region is no longer live once its script runs (`aria-live="off"`), which may change what the empty region does to this test; the next CI run will show.
- Focus moves to the next item's title heading, then the previous item's, then the list heading. (The heading is named by the title; `aria-labelledby` on an `<li>` is discouraged.) The empty state, which the server renders hidden whenever there are items, is shown after the last deletion. When there are older links beyond the 50, it says to reload to see them, and the limit note reads "This list shows only your 50 most recent links", which stays true as items are removed. The live region is `#list-status`, which is on the page on every load.
- Cancel and Escape rely on the native dialog to return focus to the Delete button.

Deleting from `/held` was added in #129:

- `/held`'s Delete forms carry `return_to=/held`. The confirmation page passes it on, shows the host after "Site:" instead of the whole address after "Address:", as `/held`'s card does, because the rest of a Held link's address may be what makes it private (decided in #129's review), its "Keep it" link reads "Keep it and go back to the links to review" and goes to `/held#bookmark-<id>`, and confirming shows `/held` with the result. Any other value, and none, returns to the saved links, so the field can't send anyone elsewhere (`deleteReturnPage`). The card's parts that both pages share, the date and the Delete form, are in `apps/worker/src/bookmark-card.ts`.
- The dialog script works on both pages. They give the heading, the live region and the empty state the same IDs, and mark each Delete button's form with `data-delete-form`, so the script, which now listens on `<main>`, leaves the address form and `/held`'s Release and Retry alone. It posts the form's own fields with `confirm=yes`, so the answer is the page the delete started from. A list left empty is removed, with its section on `/held`, focus moves as before (the next card's title, then the previous one's, then the page heading), and the empty state appears once no Delete form is left.

VoiceOver tests (#56, `apps/worker/e2e/delete-dialog.voiceover.ts`) record what it says, on the `macos-26` runner with Playwright's WebKit. The wording below comes from CI logs of exploratory runs with two saved links (run 38944739013 for the confirm step). The committed tests save one link, so the list is one item long and, after a deletion, focus goes to the list heading ("heading level 1 Saved links main", run 38946495256). The tests match only parts of the phrases:

- Opening the dialog: "Delete this link? [title] web dialog with 5 items heading level 2 Delete this link?". The dialog's name and the title come before any button, and "Cancel" is never first. The permanence notice, which is the description, is not read, which is the reason the title is in the name (#48).
- The first Tab: "Cancel button", usually followed by a hint ("You are currently on a button, inside of a web dialog. …"). The spoken log sometimes misses the short "Cancel button" and keeps only the hint, so the test checks the item under the VoiceOver cursor and the focused element as well.
- Escape: the dialog closes and VoiceOver reads the Delete button that opened it, for example "Delete [title] button list 2 items" ("list 1 item" with one link).
- Confirming: "Deleted “[title]”." is spoken when it is written 500 ms after the dialog closed and focus moved, as described above. Focus reads the next item's heading ("heading level 2 link [next title]") when there is a next item, and otherwise the list heading. The sampling is too coarse to tell which of the two is spoken first.

Deletion is not exposed to agents in Milestone 4. The MCP and WebMCP tools are search-only.

## Agent access (Milestone 4)

Both agent surfaces call the same `searchBookmarks(userId, query, { channel, inputMode })` service as the web page, so ranking, thresholds, logging, and the exclusion of Held bookmarks behave identically. `channel` is recorded on each search run, so agent searches can be analyzed separately from human searches.

**Tool design.** One tool, `search_bookmarks`, takes a natural-language `query` string and returns the ranked results: title, description, URL, and kind. It does not return enrichment internals, probabilities, or anything else from the database. The tool description tells the agent what Haystack contains and that results are the signed-in user's own saved resources. The result shape is defined once, as a Valibot schema in `packages/schemas`, and shared by both surfaces.

**WebMCP.** The search page registers the tool with the browser's WebMCP API when it is available (feature-detected, see Q9). The tool calls the existing `/api/search` endpoint with the page's session, so it needs no new authentication and only works while the user is signed in with the page open. Because WebMCP is in-browser and human-present, it is the lower-risk surface and is built first.

**Remote MCP server.** A `/mcp` endpoint on the same Worker implements the 2026-07-28 specification with its stateless core: there is no initialization handshake and no session, so every request is self-contained and handled like any other Worker request. It exposes only the search tool. Authorization follows the MCP specification (see Q8). Each MCP request resolves to a Haystack user, is checked against `ALLOWED_EMAILS` like a session, and is subject to a per-user rate limit, because agents can issue searches far faster than a person.

Write tools (saving and deleting through agents) are out of scope for Milestone 4 and listed in the backlog.

**Untrusted text in tool results.** Search-only does not mean the risk is small for the user. Titles and descriptions in tool results come from web pages, and the agent that reads them may have other tools, such as email, file access, or a shell. A bookmarked page with an injected instruction in its description then reaches that agent. Haystack cannot control the agent. It can return results as structured data (fields, not prose), keep descriptions length-capped, and state in the tool description that the result fields are third-party page text and are not instructions. Record this risk in the documentation for connecting an MCP client.

## Authentication

### Site sign-in

The site uses the Google OpenID Connect authorization code flow with PKCE and a `state` parameter. The callback exchanges the code, verifies the ID token (issuer, audience, expiry, signature against Google's published keys, and `email_verified`), then checks the email against the `ALLOWED_EMAILS` allowlist. Users are keyed on the `sub` claim, not the email address, because the email address can change.

On success, the server creates a session with a random ID, stores only its SHA-256 hash in `sessions`, and sets the raw ID in a cookie with the `__Host-` prefix and the `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, `SameSite=Lax`, and `Path=/` attributes. Every state-changing request also checks the `Origin` header as CSRF protection alongside `SameSite`. Sign-out deletes the session row and clears the cookie.

Sessions last 30 days and slide: a request in the second half of a session's life extends its expiry, so the database is not written on every request. Every request also checks the session user's email against `ALLOWED_EMAILS`, so removing an address takes effect immediately rather than when the session expires. The allowlist check needs the email stored on the user row, which is refreshed at each sign-in. The session lookup deletes an expired row it finds, and the scheduled handler deletes the rest from Milestone 2.

Every page and API route except `/auth/*` requires a session. Unauthenticated page requests and form submissions redirect to sign-in, and unauthenticated API requests return 401. A page request is a GET or HEAD whose `Accept` header includes `text/html`, and a form submission is a POST whose `Accept` header includes it (#34). Every other request counts as an API request. A browser asks for HTML when it navigates, which it does to load a page or to submit a form, while the site's scripts never ask for it: the address form's script asks for `application/json`, and the delete dialog's sends `fetch()`'s default, `*/*`. So a script still gets a 401 it can act on, and a script added later must not ask for HTML. See "Sessions that end before a form is sent" under "Return targets". Static assets (CSS, JavaScript, fonts, images) are public, and must never contain user data. Implemented in #25:

- The cookie is `__Host-session`. The session ID is 32 random bytes, base64url-encoded, and the stored hash is its hex SHA-256.
- `ALLOWED_EMAILS` is a comma-separated `vars` entry, matched without regard to ASCII case. Only A to Z are folded, so a non-ASCII lookalike does not match.
- The `Origin` check applies to every method other than GET, HEAD, and OPTIONS, including `/auth/*`.
- Sign-out is `POST /auth/sign-out`, which redirects to `/auth/signed-out`.

By default, Workers serves a matching static asset without running the Worker. Keep all HTML out of the assets directory so that every page goes through the Worker. If an HTML route must ever live in the assets directory, configure Wrangler to run the Worker first for that path (verify the current `run_worker_first` configuration) and add a test proving it redirects without a session.

Implemented in #26:

- **Redirect URI:** `<origin>/auth/callback`, from the request's own origin, in both the authorization and the token request.
- **Scope:** `openid email`.
- **No nonce:** OIDC Core makes it optional for the code flow, and `oauth4webapi` then rejects a token that carries one.
- **Login CSRF protection** (RFC 9700, section 4.7.1): sign-in sets `__Host-auth_flow` to the `state` (10 minutes, `SameSite=Lax`, so it survives Google's redirect back). The callback rejects a `state` that doesn't match the cookie before it uses up the flow, and clears the cookie on every outcome. Starting sign-in in a second tab replaces the cookie, so finishing the first tab fails, and the user signs in again.
- **A successful sign-in deletes the browser's previous session**, if it had one.
- **Expired `auth_flows` rows** are deleted whenever a new flow is created.

### Return targets

When an unauthenticated request needs sign-in, the path and query to return to are stored server-side with the OAuth `state`, not passed through as a free-form parameter. They go in the `auth_flows` table together with the PKCE verifier. A cookie is not used for this, because a `/save` return target holds the captured page data and can exceed the roughly 4 KB that browsers allow for one cookie. An `auth_flows` row is used once, deleted in the callback, and rejected after it expires. The session lookup deletes expired rows it finds, and the scheduled handler cleans up the remaining ones from Milestone 2. Before redirecting after sign-in, the server confirms that the target is a relative path on the Haystack origin. Anything else falls back to `/`. The check resolves the target against a placeholder origin. It then also rejects a resolved path that starts with `//`, because dot segments collapse during parsing (`/.//host` becomes `//host`). This prevents the sign-in flow from becoming an open redirect.

`/auth/sign-in` also screens a return target whose path is `/save` before it stores it, the way a signed-out `/save` is screened. A save link the form would read therefore has no flagged secret written to `auth_flows`, whichever way the user arrives: through `/save`, or through a hand-built sign-in link. The path is compared after `safeReturnTarget` has resolved dot segments and removed tabs, so it is the path the router compares. The stored target is rebuilt from the validated fields the form reads. A save link that the form would refuse or reject falls back to `/`, like a target off the origin, rather than ending the sign-in, because the request is still a request to sign in. The person then lands on the recent saves, which have the address form, without the page that would have said what was wrong with the link. But only a hand-built link reaches this, because Haystack's own redirect is screened first (see "Where the signed-out check runs" under "Sensitive URLs").

A save link with no captured field, such as a bare `/save`, is stored as `/save` (#97). It holds no value, so nothing that could be a secret is written, and after sign-in it shows the address form. Any query or fragment it had is dropped, because none of it is a field the form reads. Before #97 it fell back to `/`, because a bare `/save` got the validation page. Other targets are stored as given, because only the save form reads an address from its query. That includes a path the router does not treat as the form, such as `/save/` or `/Save`, which answers 404 after sign-in: a secret in its query is stored, but nothing reads it, and only the person who built the link could have put it there.

**Sessions that end before a form is sent (#34).** A session can end between loading a form and sending it: it expires, the user signs out in another tab, or their address is taken off `ALLOWED_EMAILS`. Before #34, every POST without a session got a plain-text 401, so a user who pressed Save lost the title and description they had confirmed, and had no way back to sign-in. Now a form submission without a session (a POST that asks for HTML, see "Site sign-in") gets a 303 to `/auth/sign-in?return_to=…`, which the browser follows with a GET, and the return target depends on the form. The Origin check still runs first, so a cross-origin POST gets its 403, never a redirect (`answer` in `apps/worker/src/index.ts`).

- **The save form returns to itself.** `signedOutSubmission` in `apps/worker/src/save.ts` rebuilds the save link from the hidden inputs and screens it with `signedOutSave`, as a signed-out `GET /save` of that link would be. A tampered or hand-built submission whose address is flagged or invalid therefore gets its page (400) with no redirect, so no `Location` header or `auth_flows` row carries it, and a flagged canonical URL is dropped. A body that is not a form carries nothing, so it returns to the address form at `/save`.
- **The title and description the user entered are kept.** The save link carries them as `confirmed_title` and `confirmed_description`, the form's own field names, after the captured fields, normalized as `POST /save` normalizes them (line breaks as LF, the title's whitespace collapsed, both trimmed), so building the target again in `/auth/sign-in` gives the same string. `GET /save` reads them, checks them as `POST /save` checks them, and shows the form with them, so the form comes back as `POST /save` would have shown it, including its error for an invalid value. A field the link does not carry starts from the prefill.
- **Why keeping them is acceptable.** They are the user's own text, and they end up where the captured title and description already go: in the sign-in redirect, in `auth_flows.return_to` until the callback uses the row or it expires, and in Cloudflare's request logs if Workers Logs is enabled, which "Save flow" accepts for v1. They are free text, which the sensitive URL check does not apply to (see "Which URLs are screened"), so a secret the user typed into the title would be stored there, as it would be in the bookmark if the save had gone through. The address, the only value the check applies to, is screened before anything is stored.
- **They must fit in the save link budget.** The whole save link, with the values, must stay within `SAVE_URL_BUDGET`, which keeps the sign-in redirect under Cloudflare's URL limit (`save-url-budget.test.ts` checks a submission at the budget, with the values full of the characters that grow most). When the values would take it over, the link carries `confirmed_values=not-kept` instead, and the form after sign-in starts from the prefill and says that they need entering again. Only a page whose captured values already fill most of the budget reaches this, such as one with a 2,000-character address.
- **What the user sees.** After sign-in, the save form has the page title "Not saved yet - Haystack", because a screen reader announces the title when the page loads but not a status message that arrives with it, as on the saved links page after a delete. After the heading, a `role="status"` region, styled as the saved links page's status message, says "This link is not saved yet. Check the title and description, then press Save." When the values were not kept, it says "This link is not saved yet, and its address is too long to keep the title and description with it. Enter them again, then press Save." An error for an invalid value comes before the message, and the title is then "Check the form", because the error is what stops the save. The messages describe the page rather than what the user did, because a hand-built `/save` link can carry the same fields, and because the session may have expired rather than been signed out. The values are not kept only when the address fills most of the budget, so the message names the address as the reason.
- **The address form returns to the save form for its address.** A posting address form without JavaScript (`signedOutAddress` in `apps/worker/src/save-address.ts`) gets what the form shown before sign-in gets for the address, because that form asks for `GET /save?url=…`: the address form with its error, or sign-in and then the save form for the address alone. Nothing is looked up before sign-in.
- **Any other form returns to the saved links.** A POST cannot be repeated by returning to its path, so a bookmark's Delete button, on the list or on the confirmation page, returns to `/` after sign-in, and nothing is deleted. The user presses Delete again there.
- **Scripts keep their 401.** The delete dialog says the user is signed out, and the address form's script shows its sign-in link, as before.

### Rules that hold everywhere

No endpoint accepts a `user_id` from the client. Pages never send `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`: with it, browsers send `Origin: null` on same-origin form posts, and the `Origin` check would reject every one. Use `strict-origin-when-cross-origin` or `same-origin`. Session IDs are compared by hash lookup, not string equality on raw values. Every query that reads or writes user data filters by the authenticated `user_id`, and a test for each endpoint proves that one user cannot read another user's bookmarks or search logs.

## Secrets and configuration

`.env.schema` declares every variable for varlock, with sensitive values resolved from 1Password. varlock resolves a schema from the directory where it runs. The root `.env.schema` holds shared items (`APP_ENV`), and `apps/worker/.env.schema` imports it with `@import(../../)` and declares the Worker's own variables and the 1Password plugin. The root `varlock load` in `quality` therefore needs no 1Password access, and CI needs no secrets. varlock and 1Password are used for local development only, and the 1Password desktop app authenticates (`allowAppAuth=true`, account `my.1password.com`). Deployed Workers read secrets set in Cloudflare, and CI needs no secrets. "Deploys" below describes the deploy procedure.

Local development goes through `varlock-wrangler`: `vp run dev` in `apps/worker` runs `varlock-wrangler dev`. varlock passes the resolved values to Wrangler through a named pipe (`--env-file` pointing at a FIFO), so secret values appear neither on disk nor in process arguments. This was verified in #8. Every item in `apps/worker/.env.schema` is sensitive unless it is marked `@public`, so varlock masks it in its output. Wrangler then loads them into its own `process.env`, so the local `workerd` processes inherit the secret in their environment, which only the same user can read. Removing that copy is suggested upstream in cloudflare/workers-sdk discussion #16197. `apps/worker/vite.config.ts` keeps the plain `cloudflare()` plugin and not varlock's Vite wrapper. The wrapper resolves every variable when the Vite configuration loads, so every build, including the build test in CI, would need 1Password. _Decided 2026-10-13 (#43, resolving #15): local development stays on `varlock-wrangler dev`. Static assets are plain, unbundled files in `apps/web/public`, with no build step. `apps/worker/wrangler.jsonc` sets `assets.directory` to that folder, which `wrangler dev` and the test harness (and so the Playwright fixtures) read. `vp build` ignores that field and copies Vite's `publicDir` instead, so `apps/worker/vite.config.ts` names the same folder, and the build test checks that the dialog script is in the output. A request for an existing file is served without running the Worker, so the Worker's headers do not apply: `apps/web/public/_headers` adds `nosniff` (the CSP stays on the HTML, where it has effect). The asset server sends `Cache-Control: public, max-age=0, must-revalidate` with an ETag, which is right for unhashed file names. `vp check` type-checks the scripts through `apps/web/tsconfig.json` (`checkJs`). A `vp dev` move would need the Vite plugin's experimental `_headers` support, so it is not planned._

`apps/worker/wrangler.jsonc` declares the secret names in `secrets.required`. Deploy fails if a required secret is missing, so the list grows with each milestone instead of listing everything up front. A secret is added to the list in the same change as the first code that reads it, even when nothing in production calls that code yet, as with `TYPESAFE_API_KEY` (#122), so the deploy fails before a caller ships rather than after. The one exception is `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`, which #8 added before Phase 2 uses it, to prove the secrets pipeline. `secrets` and `flagship` are not inherited by Wrangler `env.*` blocks, so if staging or production become named environments, each block repeats both.

Because `secrets` is declared, Wrangler also reads secrets from `process.env` in local dev and in the test harness. A test that needs a secret sets it through the harness's `secrets` option. Run `vp test` without varlock, so the real values never reach the tests. The harness does not bind a required secret that it cannot find, so the value is `undefined` even though the generated `Env` type says `string`.

```jsonc
{
  // Milestone 1: Capture
  "secrets": {
    "required": ["GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET"],
  },
  // Milestone 2: Enrichment adds (the TypeSafe key also powers the sensitivity check)
  //   "TYPESAFE_API_KEY" (added in #122), "ANTHROPIC_API_KEY" (added in #124),
  //   "GITHUB_TOKEN" (added in #123), "AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN" (added in #125)
  //
  // Post-v1, with the OpenAI and OpenRouter adapters:
  //   "OPENAI_API_KEY", "OPENROUTER_API_KEY"
}
```

`TYPESAFE_API_KEY` (#122), `GITHUB_TOKEN` (#123) and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` (#124) are in `apps/worker/.env.schema` as required items, like `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`. Each change added its item as optional with no value, because the key had no 1Password item yet. Since 2026-10-23, all three come from one 1Password item in the `dev` vault, referenced by its ID (`op://dev/be344swl54q6yloegahiahdrse/<field>`), so renaming the item doesn't break the references. Its fields are `TYPESAFE_AI_JEV`, `GITHUB_PAT` and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`. All three are in `secrets.required`, so each must be set in Cloudflare before the next deploy, with the `wrangler secret put` command under "Deploys", run once per secret with that secret's name in place of `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`. The command reads the secret's reference from `apps/worker/.env.schema`. "GitHub enrichment" records the settings the token is created with. The key must be scoped to a single workspace when it is created, because Anthropic requires an `anthropic-workspace-id` header for a key that isn't, and the adapter sends none (see "The provider interface"). `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` (`https://api.anthropic.com`) and `ANTHROPIC_MODEL` (`claude-haiku-5-5`) are `vars`.

`AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` (#125), the AI Gateway's authentication token, is in `secrets.required` and in `apps/worker/.env.schema`, as a required item. It is the `AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` field of the same 1Password item as the other three, referenced by the item's ID. It must be set in Cloudflare before the next deploy (see "Deploys"). The gateway's configuration is in `vars`: `AI_GATEWAY_BASE_URL` (`https://gateway.ai.cloudflare.com`, a `var` so tests can point it at the fixture), `AI_GATEWAY_ACCOUNT_ID` (`369e0fbadd08f6f15efd9fb63c7f5193`, the Cloudflare account's ID, which isn't a secret) and `AI_GATEWAY_ID` (`haystack`). The gateway passes requests on to Anthropic, so it uses `ANTHROPIC_MODEL` and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` too (see "The provider interface"). `ENRICHMENT_PROVIDER`, the setting that chooses the provider by its `id`, is a `var` set to `ai-gateway`. The token is scoped to the account, not to the gateway: the Authentication page says "Any token with `AI Gateway Run` can send requests through every gateway in the account, including any configured with stored provider keys through BYOK". Haystack has one gateway and stores no keys in it, so this is harmless today, but a gateway added to the account later is reachable with the same token.

Non-secret static configuration, such as the base URLs for Jev, the LLM providers, GitHub, and Google (so integration tests can point them at fixtures), the AI Gateway account and gateway IDs, the model chosen for each provider, the sweep's attempt limit (`SWEEP_ATTEMPT_LIMIT`, #128), `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD`, `GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID`, and `ALLOWED_EMAILS`, goes in `vars`. Never run `wrangler secret put` with a value in the command line.

**The sweep's frequency is not a `var`** (#128). A `var` can't change a Cron Trigger's schedule, so the frequency is the schedule itself, the `triggers.crons` entry in `apps/worker/wrangler.jsonc`, which started at every 5 minutes (`*/5 * * * *`, Q3) and is hourly (`0 * * * *`) since #133, once the backlog was empty. Changing it is a reviewed change that goes through a deploy, as changing a `var` is.

**Runtime switches** are Flagship flags (decision 20): the search questions (`both`, `noul`, or `score`), the display signal, the Noul and Score thresholds, the request mode, whether the sweep is enabled (the boolean flag `sweep-enabled`), and the sweep batch size (the number flag `sweep-batch-size`). These are the settings that change during evaluation and operation. `sweep-enabled` stops only the sweep: until #199 is done, it doesn't stop POST /save's on-save path (#133). `SENSITIVE_WARN_THRESHOLD` (`0.5`, which Schalk chose, added in #126) and `ALLOWED_EMAILS` stay in `vars` on purpose. Changing the sensitivity threshold changes which bookmarks are Held, and changing the allowlist changes who can sign in, so both should be reviewed changes that go through a deploy. `ENRICHMENT_PROVIDER` (#125) stays in `vars` for the same reason: changing it changes which third party receives every bookmark's data.

The Worker reads flags through a binding, declared as follows (field names checked against the Wrangler 4.147.0 configuration schema):

```jsonc
{
  "flagship": [{ "binding": "FLAGS", "app_id": "<APP_ID>" }],
}
```

One configuration module reads every flag once per request, or once per sweep run, and passes the values on. No other code calls the binding. Each flag's default value is a constant in that module, so the defaults are reviewed in code. The typed getters return the default for a missing flag, a flag that cannot be evaluated, or a type mismatch, and they throw for an empty or non-string key and for any other error (see Q13). The module therefore uses constant, non-empty keys, and it catches any error from the binding and uses the defaults, so a Flagship outage means the Worker runs with those defaults. The defaults are the safe values: both questions, the Score signal, batched mode, and the sweep enabled with a batch size of 3. The ceiling on the batch size is `SWEEP_BATCH_SIZE_LIMIT`, which #130 kept at 3 (Q3). The default must never be above it, because `enrichPending` throws a `RangeError` for a larger batch, and `runScheduled` records that as a failed sweep step, so during a Flagship outage, which runs with the defaults, every run would fail. The module also checks the live flag: a value that isn't a whole number from 1 to the ceiling is replaced by the default, so a mistyped flag can't make `enrichPending` throw either (#128). The same fallback applies while `sweep-enabled` is off: a Flagship outage then gives the default, the sweep enabled with a batch of 3, so every scheduled run during the outage can enrich up to 3 bookmarks while the flag says the sweep is off, for example during Phase 9's review of the first run. _Accepted 2026-10-26 by Schalk (#173):_ the risk is low and manageable. The worst case is paid requests, and enrichments that Phase 9's review hasn't checked, which raising `schema_version` enriches again. The window lasts only until the reviewed run. Deploying without the Cron Trigger until then was the alternative.

_Built 2026-10-26 (#128)._ The configuration module is `apps/worker/src/flags.ts`. So far it reads the sweep's two flags, with `readSweepFlags`, once for each scheduled run. The search flags join it with Phase 11. It reads them through the details getters (`getBooleanDetails` and `getNumberDetails`), because a missing flag gives its default without throwing, and the default for `sweep-enabled` turns the sweep on. So when a getter reports an `errorCode`, the run logs `Flag not evaluated, using its default:` with the flag's key and the code, and never the getter's `errorMessage`. A code that isn't a plain upper-case code is logged as `UNRECOGNIZED`. A missing `sweep-batch-size` isn't logged, because that flag is created only to lower the batch without a deploy (see "Deploys"), so its absence is the expected state, and a warning on every run would bury the ones that matter.

- **The keys** are `sweep-enabled`, a boolean flag, and `sweep-batch-size`, a number flag, as the Flagship app must name them. The local simulator accepts a key of 1 to 64 letters, digits, `-` and `_`, and both fit.
- **The defaults** are `SWEEP_FLAG_DEFAULTS`: enabled, and a batch of `SWEEP_BATCH_SIZE`, 3, from `enrich-pending.ts`. The ceiling is `SWEEP_BATCH_SIZE_LIMIT` from the same module, so the flag and `enrichPending` can't disagree about it.
- **A Flagship error** gives both defaults, even when one of the two reads worked, because the two are read together and a run should see one consistent configuration. The error is logged by its code or name only. A missing binding counts as an error.
- **A live value out of range** gives that flag's default, with a warning that names the flag but not its value: a batch size that isn't a whole number from 1 to 3, such as 4, 0 or 2.5, and a switch that isn't a boolean. The getters shouldn't return the wrong type, but Flagship is an outside service, so its answers are untrusted input.
- **Tests** are in `apps/worker/test/flags.test.ts`, with a fake binding typed to the part of the binding the module calls (`FlagReader`), because the harness can't set a flag (Q13). They don't use the internal `FlagshipBinding::admin_api`.

### Deploys

**The deploy command is `vp -C apps/worker run deploy`, which runs `vp build && wrangler deploy` in `apps/worker` (#16).** Every command in this section runs from the repository root, as in "Backups and restore" under "Data model". The Cloudflare Vite plugin writes the built Worker and its Wrangler configuration to `dist/haystack/`, and writes `.wrangler/deploy/config.json`, which points at that configuration. Plain `wrangler deploy` follows the pointer and reports "Using redirected Wrangler configuration". Building in the same command means a deploy never uploads an old build. Git ignores both folders. `vp -C apps/worker run deploy --dry-run` builds and bundles the Worker and prints its bindings without contacting Cloudflare, because the script passes the flag on to Wrangler. It does not check that the secret is set in Cloudflare, so it cannot stand in for the first-deploy check below.

**Never run `varlock-wrangler deploy`.** varlock's Wrangler wrapper resolves `apps/worker/.env.schema` and hands every sensitive value to Wrangler through `--secrets-file`, so it would upload the local development secrets to the deployed Worker (`handleDeploy` in `@varlock/cloudflare-integration` 1.6.1). Plain `wrangler deploy` uploads a secret only when it is given `--secrets-file`. It turns `.env`, `.dev.vars` and `process.env` into Worker bindings only for local development (`getVarsForDev` in Wrangler 4.149.0). On a deploy it reads `.env` only for its own settings, such as an API token. varlock is for local development (`vp run dev` in `apps/worker`) only.

**Run the deploy from a shell that does not have a secret in its environment, and keep `apps/worker` free of `.env` and `.dev.vars` files.** `vp build` copies any `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` it finds in either place into `dist/haystack/.dev.vars`, for `vite preview`, and the file stays there in plain text until the next build. Plain `wrangler deploy` does not upload it, but it is the secret on disk. The build's warning about the missing secret suggests adding it to `.dev.vars` or `.env`, so ignore that warning: the secret belongs in Cloudflare, not the build.

**Secrets are set in Cloudflare with `wrangler secret put <NAME>`, one at a time, or in the Cloudflare dashboard.** The command has no option for the value. In a terminal it asks with a masked prompt, and otherwise it reads the value from standard input. Never put a value on the command line, where shell history and the process list keep it. Piping it from 1Password means it is never typed, copied to the clipboard or written to disk. Wrangler trims the trailing newline that `op read` prints. Log in first with `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler login`, and check with `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler whoami`, because the piped command has no terminal, so it cannot open the browser to log in. For another secret, replace `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` in both places with its name.

```sh
(
  ref=$(sed -n 's/^GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET=op("\(.*\)")$/\1/p' apps/worker/.env.schema) &&
    [ -n "$ref" ] &&
    secret=$(op read "$ref") &&
    [ -n "${secret//[[:space:]]/}" ] &&
    printf '%s' "$secret" | vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler secret put GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET
)
```

**The command reads the value once and refuses an empty one, because `wrangler secret put` stores an empty secret without complaint.** In a plain pipe, the right-hand command runs even when `op read` fails, for example because 1Password is locked or the reference does not resolve, and then Wrangler reads empty input. A 1Password field that is empty is a second case, because `op read` prints nothing and still exits successfully. In Wrangler 4.149.0 the Worker's `secret put` handler trims the input and uploads whatever is left, while the Secrets Store `secret create` handler throws "Need to pass in a value when creating a secret." for an empty value. `secrets.required` does not help, because it only checks that the secret exists. The first production deploy stored an empty `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` this way, and every sign-in failed (#98). The command stores the value in a shell variable first, and only starts Wrangler when the reference was found, `op read` succeeded, and the value has at least one character that is not whitespace. Wrangler trims the value, so one made only of whitespace would also be stored empty. Each step must succeed before the next runs, so a missing reference or a failed `op read` stores nothing. The parentheses run it all in a subshell, so `ref` and `secret` disappear when it ends, and its exit status is the status of the upload: a failure anywhere is a non-zero status, not a quiet success. It works in zsh and bash, which both support `${secret//[[:space:]]/}`. This is safe for the value because `printf` is a shell builtin, so the value never appears in the process list, and shell history records the command text, not the value. The `sed` expression takes the reference from `apps/worker/.env.schema`, which holds references and no values.

**Varlock can resolve the value instead of `op read`.** `varlock printenv <NAME>` (varlock 1.22.0) prints the resolved value of one item in `apps/worker/.env.schema`, through the same 1Password plugin, so it needs no `sed` for the reference. The same safeguards apply: the value is stored once, refused if empty, and piped to Wrangler. Run it from the repository root:

```sh
(
  secret=$(vp -C apps/worker exec varlock printenv AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN) &&
    [ -n "${secret//[[:space:]]/}" ] &&
    printf '%s' "$secret" | vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler secret put AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN
)
```

`AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` was set this way on 2026-10-24. Don't use `varlock-wrangler` for this. In `@varlock/cloudflare-integration` 1.6.1 it has no command that sets one secret: `deploy`, `versions upload` and `preview` upload every sensitive item at once, which is why `varlock-wrangler deploy` is ruled out above, and every other command, `secret put` included, is passed to Wrangler unchanged (`main` in `varlock-wrangler.mjs`).

**After setting a secret, check that it works.** The Worker has no `observability` setting, so Cloudflare stores no logs, and the log line is only visible live. Start `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler tail haystack` (or the dashboard's live log stream) before the sign-in. For `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`, start a sign-in at `/auth/sign-in`, which must reach Google's account page, and complete it there. The page that follows must not be "Sign-in failed". If it is, the log shows `Sign-in failed: GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET_EMPTY` for an empty secret, and any other code points at a different cause.

**The first deploy needs the secret set before the Worker exists.** Because of `secrets.required`, a deploy fails when a listed secret is not set. For a Worker that does not exist yet, Wrangler uploads the static assets and then stops with an error, before it creates the Worker. Its message suggests `wrangler deploy --secrets-file`. Don't follow that suggestion, because it means writing the secret to a file. Run `wrangler secret put` first instead. When no Worker has the configured name, it asks whether to create one. Without a terminal, as in the pipe above, it creates the Worker without asking. The Worker is a placeholder whose fetch handler returns nothing, so any request to it fails, and it holds the secret and no data. The first deploy replaces it. On the first deploy, run `vp -C apps/worker run deploy` once before setting the secret, to confirm that it fails and that no Worker named `haystack` exists afterwards (`vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler deployments list`, or the dashboard). That is the check #16 asks for.

**A secret added later** (Milestone 2 adds four) is set with `wrangler secret put` before deploying the change that adds it to `secrets.required`. `wrangler secret put` redeploys the code that is already deployed, with the new secret, which is harmless because that code does not read it yet. For a Worker that already exists, Cloudflare rejects a deploy that is missing a listed secret, and Wrangler reports the missing names.

**The AI Gateway must exist before the next deploy (#125).** `AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` is in `secrets.required`, so the deploy fails until it is set, and the token comes from the gateway. Do this once, in this order:

1. In the Cloudflare dashboard, go to **AI** > **AI Gateway**, select **Create Gateway**, name it `haystack` (the `AI_GATEWAY_ID` in `vars`), and choose **Standard billing** under **Workers AI Billing**.
2. In the gateway's **Settings**, leave **Cache Responses** and **Retry Requests** off, leave **Logs** on, and turn on **Require provider credentials**. The adapter's one-request rule (Q3) depends on Retry Requests staying off, and Q7's metadata-only logging depends on Logs being on and the adapter's header. Require provider credentials makes the gateway refuse, with a `400`, any request it would otherwise bill to Cloudflare credits, as the adapter's `cf-aig-no-wholesale` header does for its own requests (see "The provider interface").
3. In the same **Settings**, select **Create authentication token**. The token is shown once, so save it in 1Password straight away, in the `AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` field of the item `apps/worker/.env.schema` already references (done on 2026-10-24).
4. Turn on **Authenticated Gateway** in the same **Settings**.
5. Set `AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` in Cloudflare with one of the `wrangler secret put` commands above, and then deploy. The gateway was created, and the token set with the Varlock command, on 2026-10-24.

**Check the gateway before the enrichment module (#126) ships.** Nothing calls the gateway until then. Two things the documentation leaves open are confirmed here rather than by the first bookmarks: that the gateway passes the Anthropic key on (a `400` or `401` would count a permanent failure against every bookmark the sweep tries), and that this endpoint honours `cf-aig-collect-log-payload` (the Logging page's examples use the REST API). Send one request with the adapter's headers and a prompt that holds no bookmark data. `printf` is a shell builtin, so the key and the token reach `curl` through its standard input (`--config -`) and never appear in the process list:

```sh
(
  key=$(op read "op://dev/be344swl54q6yloegahiahdrse/ANTHROPIC_API_KEY") &&
  token=$(op read "op://dev/be344swl54q6yloegahiahdrse/AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN") &&
  printf 'header = "x-api-key: %s"\nheader = "cf-aig-authorization: Bearer %s"\n' "$key" "$token" |
    curl --config - -sS -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' \
      -H "anthropic-version: 2026-10-21" -H "content-type: application/json" \
      -H "cf-aig-collect-log-payload: false" -H "cf-aig-skip-cache: true" -H "cf-aig-no-wholesale: true" \
      -d '{"model":"claude-haiku-5-5","max_tokens":256,"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"Reply with OK."}]}' \
      "https://gateway.ai.cloudflare.com/v1/369e0fbadd08f6f15efd9fb63c7f5193/haystack/anthropic/v1/messages"
)
```

It prints only the status. A `200` confirms that the gateway passed the key on. Then open the gateway's logs: the entry should show the model, the status, the tokens and the cost, with no request or response body. If either check fails, set `ENRICHMENT_PROVIDER` to `anthropic` (see below) until it is resolved. Keep `anthropic-version` and the model in step with `ANTHROPIC_VERSION` and `ANTHROPIC_MODEL`.

_The request was sent on 2026-10-24, with Authenticated Gateway and Require provider credentials on._ With the key in `authorization: Bearer`, which the adapter first sent, the gateway answered `400` with code `2044`, "Customer-provided provider credentials are required for this request". With the key in `x-api-key`, as the command above and the adapter now send it, it answered `200` (see "The provider interface"). The `200`'s log entry, read in the dashboard on the same day, has the model, the status, the tokens (15 in, 4 out), the cost ($0.0000035) and the duration, and no request or response body. It also shows `"wholesale": false` (billed to Haystack's Anthropic key, not Cloudflare credits), `"retry_count": 0` and `"cached": false`. So the gateway keeps no bodies for this endpoint, which is what Q7 requires. One entry can't show whether that is the header's doing or how the gateway always logs this endpoint, so the adapter keeps sending the header.

**Switching between the gateway and Anthropic directly** is a one-line change: set `ENRICHMENT_PROVIDER` in `apps/worker/wrangler.jsonc` to `anthropic` or `ai-gateway`, and deploy. Both adapters are always built and run the same contract test, both use `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `ANTHROPIC_MODEL`, and `AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` stays in `secrets.required` either way, so a switch needs no other change. The enrichment row's `provider` column records which one wrote each row.

**Watching the cost.** Anthropic bills every request, through the gateway or not, and its Console's usage and cost pages are the accurate figures. The gateway's **Analytics** show requests, tokens, errors and cost, but the [Costs](https://developers.cloudflare.com/ai-gateway/observability/costs/) page calls its cost "an **estimation** based on the number of tokens sent and received", and says to "refer to your provider's dashboard for the most accurate cost details". To cap spending, use the gateway's [Spend limits](https://developers.cloudflare.com/ai-gateway/features/spend-limits/): when a rule's budget is reached, "AI Gateway blocks further requests with a `429` response", which the adapter classifies as transient, so the sweep stops early and the bookmarks stay Pending rather than failing. Anthropic's own limits also work ([Rate limits](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/api/rate-limits) and [Workspaces](https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/workspaces)): the tier's monthly cap answers `429`, which is transient, and a spend limit set for the organization or a workspace answers `400` (`invalid_request_error`, "You have reached your specified API usage limits"). Since #154, the adapters recognize that `400` and the sweep stops its run for it without counting it against the bookmark (see "Enrichment sweep"), so either limit pauses enrichment. That depends on Anthropic's message wording, which no field backs, so the gateway's limit is still the steadier choice. The Spend limits page describes no alert as a budget is approached, and Anthropic's Workspaces page doesn't say how its threshold alerts are delivered, so check both dashboards now and then. All five pages were read on 2026-10-24.

**The sweep's flag must be off before the next deploy (#128).** The deploy adds the Cron Trigger, which runs the sweep every 5 minutes from the moment it is live. A missing flag gives the configuration module's default, which is the sweep enabled (see "Secrets and configuration"), so without the flag the first deploy would start draining the backlog straight away, while Phase 9 (#130) wants a single first run that Schalk reviews. Do this once, before the deploy:

1. Log in with `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler login`, and check with `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler whoami`.
2. Create the flag in the Flagship app, as a boolean flag that serves off:

   ```sh
   vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler flagship flags create 0166d45b-a0b5-41e5-9eef-53c0b9987d81 sweep-enabled \
     --type boolean --default off --remote --description "Whether the scheduled run enriches bookmarks (#128)"
   ```
