   A boolean flag gets the variations `on` (`true`) and `off` (`false`). `--remote` is what the command does without `--local` too, but it is written out so the command can't touch the local flag store by mistake. The command is in open beta in Wrangler 4.151.0, where its options were checked.

3. Check that it serves off: `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler flagship flags get 0166d45b-a0b5-41e5-9eef-53c0b9987d81 sweep-enabled --remote` should list `off` as the default, with no rules.
4. Deploy. In the tail, each run should log `Enrichment sweep skipped:`, which also shows that the runs read the flag (#130 records whether they do), and then `Expired rows deleted:`, because the cleanup doesn't depend on the flag. A `Flag not evaluated, using its default:` line for `sweep-enabled` with `FLAG_NOT_FOUND` means the Worker can't see the flag, for example because it was created in another app, and the sweep is running.

`sweep-batch-size` doesn't need to be created. A missing flag gives the default of 3, which is Q3's starting size and its ceiling, so creating the flag at 3 would change nothing today. It would also hide a later change to the default in `apps/worker/src/flags.ts`, which is reviewed in code, behind a value set in Flagship. Create it only when the batch size has to be lowered without a deploy, as Q3 describes for a run that comes close to its CPU limit.

The flag stops only the sweep. Since #133, POST /save enriches each new bookmark itself, and until #199 is done, switching `sweep-enabled` off doesn't stop that (see "The on-save path" under "Enrichment sweep"). Phase 9 (#130) turns the flag on for the single first run and off again, and later on for good, with `vp -C apps/worker exec wrangler flagship flags set 0166d45b-a0b5-41e5-9eef-53c0b9987d81 sweep-enabled --variation on --remote` (and `--variation off`).

**The order for each deploy:**

1. `main` passes `quality`.
2. Take the export with `vp -C apps/worker run db:export:remote`, then apply the migrations with `vp -C apps/worker run db:migrate:remote` (see "Backups and restore" under "Data model"). Migrations go first, because every migration stays compatible with the Worker version that is already deployed.
3. Run `vp -C apps/worker run deploy`.

**Production shares the local development Google OAuth client.** _Decided 2026-10-18 (#16)._ Haystack has one user, so one client keeps the Google setup in one place:

- The production redirect URI, `https://<worker>.<subdomain>.workers.dev/auth/callback`, is added to the client next to `http://localhost:5173/auth/callback`.
- `GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID` stays a single value in `vars`.
- Cloudflare holds the same secret as the 1Password item.

The cost is that production and local development share one secret. The development copy passes through the local Wrangler processes' environment (see above), so a leak there is a leak of the production secret too. Sharing also leaves `http://localhost:5173/auth/callback` as a valid redirect for production sign-in. That is harmless, because sign-in uses PKCE and keeps its state on the server (`auth_flows`), so an authorization code sent to a local port is useless without the verifier and the secret. If the secret leaks:

1. Rotate the secret in Google Cloud Console.
2. Update the 1Password item.
3. Set the new value with `wrangler secret put`.

A separate production client becomes worth having if anyone else runs a development copy.

## Dependency updates

Dependabot proposes npm and GitHub Actions version updates weekly, and waits 7 days after a release before proposing it (`.github/dependabot.yml`). The schedule and the wait apply to version updates only: a security update can arrive at any time. Some packages only work at matching versions, but Dependabot updates each package in its own pull request unless a group says otherwise (#60).

- **Groups.** Each group below exists twice, once for version updates and once for security updates, because a group covers only version updates unless it says otherwise.
  - `wrangler` and `@cloudflare/vite-plugin`: the plugin checks that the installed Wrangler matches the version it requires, and fails the build otherwise.
  - `varlock` and `@varlock/*`: each plugin requires the `varlock` release it shipped with, and imports its internals.
  - `@guidepup/*`: `@guidepup/playwright` declares the lowest `@guidepup/guidepup` it works with as a peer dependency, which pnpm warns about but doesn't enforce (#178), and `@guidepup/setup` installs the VoiceOver assets that the installed `@guidepup/guidepup` lists (see "Screen reader tests"). The group's name starts with `guidepup`, so the VoiceOver workflow runs on its pull requests (#185).
- **Calavera manages `.github/dependabot.yml`,** but cannot express groups yet (create-project-calavera#691), so the groups were added by hand with approval. Until that issue is resolved, re-add the groups after any `calavera apply` that rewrites the file.
- **A Wrangler update can also need the Worker types regenerated.** Wrangler bundles the Workers runtime (workerd), and `apps/worker/worker-configuration.d.ts` records the runtime version it was generated from. When an update moves the runtime, `types:check` fails in CI ("Types at worker-configuration.d.ts are out of date", as in #52 and #109). The Dependabot Worker types workflow (`.github/workflows/dependabot-worker-types.yml`, #9) regenerates the file on Dependabot pull requests that change the lockfile, and the manual steps below are the backup for when it fails.
  - **What the workflow does.** It runs in two jobs, because installing the updated dependencies runs their code. The first job checks out the pull request's head commit with a read-only token, installs, runs `vp run @haystack/worker#types`, and uploads the file only when it changed. The second job never checks out or runs the pull request's code. It holds the only token that can write, checks that the artifact is that one file, under 2 MiB, valid UTF-8 without control or format characters, and starts with Wrangler's header, and commits it to the pull request's branch with the GraphQL `createCommitOnBranch` mutation, which GitHub signs. The mutation's `expectedHeadOid` is the commit the first job checked out, so GitHub refuses the commit if the branch moved in between. When the types did not change, the second job does not run, and nothing is committed.
  - **Dependabot keeps rebasing.** Dependabot stops rebasing a pull request once someone else commits to it, unless the commit message contains `[dependabot skip]`, which the workflow's commit headline does. A rebase drops the commit, and the workflow then regenerates the types again.
  - **The next Quality run needs approval.** A commit made with the workflow's `GITHUB_TOKEN` starts the pull request's next runs in an "approval required" state, so approve them once from the pull request's checks (**Approve workflows to run**). The workflow runs again too, and commits nothing, because the types now match.
  - **The first real run is the end-to-end check.** #109, or the next Dependabot pull request that changes the types, shows whether the workflow works. #109 will not start the workflow by itself, because a merge commit pushed to its branch (da9430e) stopped Dependabot from rebasing it. After the workflow merges, comment `@dependabot rebase` on #109, or select **Update branch**, to start it. That run also confirms the downloaded artifact's file name: the download takes it from the response's `Content-Disposition` header, and if it is not `worker-configuration.d.ts`, the artifact check fails, and nothing is committed.
  - **Manual steps.** If the workflow fails, or a pull request needs the types regenerated outside it, check out the pull request's branch, run `vp install` and then `vp run @haystack/worker#types`, check the result with `vp run @haystack/worker#types:check`, and commit `apps/worker/worker-configuration.d.ts` to that branch. Include `[dependabot skip]` in the commit message so that Dependabot can still rebase the pull request.
- **`vite-plus` and the `vite` alias cannot be grouped.** The workspace catalog in `pnpm-workspace.yaml` pins `vite` to `npm:@voidzero-dev/vite-plus-core@…`, the Vite build that Vite+ ships, so that the Cloudflare plugin and Vite+ use the same Vite. Each `vite-plus` release requires exactly the same `vite-plus-core` version, but Dependabot does not update the alias. In #51, its commit moved `vite-plus` to 1.1.0 and left the alias at 1.0.0, which installed two copies of Vite. (dependabot-core#15847, mainly about Bun, notes that version updates skip aliased packages in npm and pnpm projects too.) So every Dependabot pull request for `vite-plus` needs a commit that sets the alias to the same version and reinstalls. A mismatch does not always fail CI: in #51 it did only because the Cloudflare plugin's types clashed. Vite+ tracks the problem in [voidzero-dev/vite-plus#2356](https://github.com/voidzero-dev/vite-plus/issues/2356), including a proposed check that fails when the two versions differ. If the manual step becomes a burden before that ships, add a Haystack check that compares the two versions.
- **`@types/node` is on major 25 (#63).** Node 24 is the LTS line and 26 is the newest release, so 25 was accepted as safe. Dependabot skips major 26 because of a `@dependabot ignore this major version` comment on its pull request (#50). That setting is stored by Dependabot, not in the repository, so `dependabot.yml` does not show it.

## Testing strategy

**User journeys** (#115) say in plain language what a person can do with Haystack, and what happens when something goes wrong. They are Gherkin scenarios in `docs/journeys/`, one `.feature` file per area, and they quote the wording a person sees and hears from the code. They are documentation and the input for the Playwright tests, and they do not run, so there is no Cucumber and no step definitions. Each scenario is tagged with the tests that cover it (`@playwright:`, `@integration:`, `@voiceover:` or `@unit:`, followed by the test file's path), or with `@untested`, and `docs/journeys/README.md` lists the untested journeys as candidates for new tests. New behaviour gets its journey written before or with its tests, and a change to behaviour or wording changes its journeys in the same pull request.

**Unit and integration tests** run with `vp test` (Vitest 5, bundled with Vite+). See Q10 (a) for the reasons behind this approach. Pure modules have plain unit tests. Integration tests start the Worker in the local Workers runtime with Wrangler's `createTestHarness`, through `createTestWorker`, which empties every secret and points every outside service at a `.test` address unless the test says otherwise (see "Every test Worker starts through `createTestWorker`" below), and use a real local D1 binding. Before running, they apply the same migration files as production with the harness's `applyD1Migrations`, so the tested schema is always the migrated schema. One test applies the migration files itself instead (`apps/worker/test/enrichment-migration.test.ts`), because it seeds Milestone 1 rows between the first migration and the enrichment migration to show that those rows survive it (#120). Jev, LLM, GitHub, and Google calls go to a small HTTP server that the test starts and that serves recorded responses. The base URLs for those services are `vars`, which the tests override. Tests cover Haystack's own behavior and decisions. They do not check that a library or tool (for example Wrangler, Vite+, Drizzle, or `oauth4webapi`) behaves as documented. A library or tool that fails or behaves unexpectedly is reported upstream, with a patch where possible, and is not worked around in silence. External services that Haystack calls at runtime (Jev, the LLM providers, GitHub, Google, and Flagship) are different: their responses are untrusted input. Haystack validates them, retries, and falls back as this plan describes, and tests cover that handling. The `evals/` contract check below compares Haystack's recorded fixtures and schemas with the live services. It is not a test of those services. No unit test calls a live paid API. Recorded fixtures can drift from the live APIs, so `evals/` also contains a small contract check. It sends one live request to each external API, validates the response with the same Valibot schemas the clients use, and runs before each milestone deploy.

**Markup validation.** The Worker renders every page (decision 1), so its pages are not `.html` files, and the `html-validate "**/*.html"` script (`lint:html`, not part of `pnpm quality`) does not see them. Integration tests render each page through the test harness and validate the result with html-validate's programmatic API and the existing `.htmlvalidate.json` configuration (issue #3). The extension's popup page, `apps/extension/index.html` (#70), is the only `.html` file, so it is the only input of `vp run lint:html`. It follows the formatter's style, a lowercase `<!doctype html>` and self-closing void elements, because `vp check --fix` rewrites it that way on every commit. `apps/extension/.htmlvalidate.json` sets `doctype-style` to `lowercase` and `void-style` to `selfclosing` for that directory only. html-validate merges it over the repository's `.htmlvalidate.json`, so the presets still apply, and the Worker's rendered pages keep the repository's style.

**End-to-end tests** use Playwright for the search page and the save form, including keyboard-only operation and an axe accessibility scan. The extension is tested in Chromium by launching a persistent context with `--load-extension`. Playwright cannot load Firefox extensions, so Firefox is covered by a documented manual check using `web-ext run`.

Set up in #11:

- The browser tests live in `apps/worker/e2e` (`*.e2e.ts`, so Vitest does not pick them up), because they test the pages the Worker renders. The extension tests (Phase 5) live with the extension.
- Each Playwright worker process starts the Worker with `createTestHarness` and applies the migrations (`e2e/fixtures.ts`), and `baseURL` is that Worker's origin. So the browser tests need no dev server and no secrets. Its `signIn` fixture (#31) seeds a signed-in user in D1, gives the browser the session, and checks that the Worker accepts it.
  - **Chromium gets a real cookie,** with the attributes the Worker sets. `addCookies` with an `http:` `url` clears `Secure`, which a `__Host-` cookie needs, so Chromium refuses it. With a `domain` and `path` instead, the cookie is accepted (#70). A header would not do for Chromium: it does not send Playwright's extra HTTP headers on the GET that follows a 303, which the address form's lookup ends with (#105).
  - **WebKit gets a `Cookie` header** that Playwright adds to every request (`setExtraHTTPHeaders`), as both browsers did before #105. WebKit keeps it on the GET after a 303. A cookie does not do for WebKit, because whether it sends a `Secure` cookie to http://127.0.0.1 depends on the macOS it runs on. On macOS 27.0.1, Playwright's WebKit build 2370 sent it, headless and headed. On the macOS 26 CI runner, the same build did not: in the trace of PR #107's VoiceOver run, `addCookies` succeeded, the first `GET /save` was answered with a redirect to sign-in, and the browser went on to Google. The VoiceOver tests on `main`, which used the header, passed on the same runner.
  - **The check** loads the signed-out page, which looks up no session, and asks for `/` with the page's own `fetch`. The Worker answers a request that is not for a page with 401 when it has no session, rather than a redirect, so a test without a session fails at once, and the browser never leaves for Google. A navigation to a signed-in page could not be stopped there, because Playwright's routes do not see the hops of a redirect. `signOut` takes the session away whichever way it was given.
  - **`e2e/session.e2e.ts`** covers the sign-in that the VoiceOver tests use: the address form's lookup and its 303, a save through the confirmation form, and the check failing for a browser with no session. It runs in Chromium with the other browser tests, and in headed WebKit as the first project of the VoiceOver configuration (`webkit-session`), which the VoiceOver tests depend on. So on the macOS runner a broken sign-in fails in seconds, without starting VoiceOver. Locally, `vp exec playwright test --config playwright.voiceover.config.ts --project webkit-session` runs it in WebKit, also without VoiceOver.
- **Jev answers through the page fixture (#132).** Since the save form asks Jev, the Worker that the browser tests start has `JEV_BASE_URL` set to `https://jev.test` (from `createTestWorker`, below) and a test key, and the page fixture answers that address, because it answers every `.test` address. Every test starts with Jev answering a noul of 0.05, below the threshold, so the save form shows no warning unless a test asks for one through the `jev` fixture (`noul(…)`, one answer per request with the last repeating, or `unavailable()`, a 503). The Worker's `reset()` sets that answer again after the page fixture forgets its routes, and both the Worker's and the extension's fixtures call it before each test. The integration tests that don't start the Jev fixture give no key, and `createTestWorker` binds an empty one whatever the shell holds, so their check finds the configuration error before it sends anything.
- **Every test Worker starts through `createTestWorker` (`apps/worker/test/test-worker.ts`, #132's review).** The harness binds more than a test's options. For the secrets in `secrets.required`, Wrangler 4.151.3's `getVarsForDev` (in `wrangler-dist/cli.js`) also reads `.dev.vars`, `.env` files and the environment of the process that started it, because it passes `includeProcessEnv: true` whenever the config has `secrets`. So with a real `TYPESAFE_API_KEY` in the shell, a test that rendered the save form without the Jev fixture bound that key, kept `wrangler.jsonc`'s production `JEV_BASE_URL`, and sent paid requests to TypeSafe, and its result depended on the answer. The review reproduced it without network access: `TYPESAFE_API_KEY=ts-dummy-env-key vp test apps/worker/test/save-sensitivity.test.ts -t "no TypeSafe key"` failed with two unplanned requests. `createTestWorker` now starts every harness, in the integration tests and in `e2e/fixtures.ts`, with two overrides, which the harness's `WorkerInput` documents as "test-only" values over the config's and the files': every name in `wrangler.jsonc`'s `secrets.required` is an empty secret, unless the test gives its own test value, and every address the Worker fetches from an outside service is under `.test`: each enrichment service's base URL is its fixture's, and Google's token endpoint and keys are `https://oauth2.google.test/token` and `https://www.google.test/oauth2/v3/certs`, unless the test gives others, as `sign-in.test.ts` does. Google's issuer and authorization endpoint stay as they are, because the Worker never fetches them. The secrets' names are read from `wrangler.jsonc` itself, so a secret added there is emptied too, and a file in which they can't be found throws rather than start a Worker. The reader takes the file's comments out first, outside strings, so a comment inside the list that holds a `]` or a quoted name can neither end the list early nor add a name (`apps/worker/test/test-worker.test.ts`). Before the change, the same exposure applied to `GITHUB_TOKEN`, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `AI_GATEWAY_TOKEN` and `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET`, though no test outside a fixture reached a request that used them: enrichment runs only in the tests that start the enrichment fixtures, and every sign-in callback outside `sign-in.test.ts` has no flow, so it stops before Google. The proof is the whole of `vp test` and both browser suites passing with dummy values for all five secrets in the environment, run with a preloaded module that refused and recorded any `fetch` to a host that is neither loopback nor under `.test`. It recorded only `https://example.com`, which `save-address.test.ts` requests on purpose to show that the page fixture fails a request it didn't plan for.
- Only Chromium is used, with one exception: `session.e2e.ts` also runs in headed WebKit, as the `webkit-session` project of the VoiceOver configuration (`playwright.voiceover.config.ts`).
- They run as part of `vp run quality` (`vp run -r test:e2e`). Before the first run, install the browser once with `vp -C apps/worker run e2e:install` (Chromium's headless shell). Do it again after a Playwright upgrade. CI installs the same browser before `quality`.
- axe runs every rule, including the best-practice rules, with no `withTags` filter. The best-practice rules (landmarks, heading order) matter for server-rendered pages.
- The Phase 6 run against production needs a different fixture.

The design's browser tests (#99, in `apps/worker/e2e/design.e2e.ts`):

- At a 320 px viewport, the signed-out page, the save form and its error state, the save form with the warning that the link might be private and after a submission without its box (#132), the saved links page with the address form, the address form after a rejected long address, and the recent saves list with its delete dialog open have no horizontal scroll (`document.documentElement.scrollWidth <= clientWidth`). The address and the title have no character a line could break at, so only `overflow-wrap` can keep them in the page (#33).
- axe runs every rule in Playwright's `colorScheme: "dark"` and `"light"`, on every page a browser test can reach: the signed-out page, the not-found page signed out and signed in, the empty list, the save form and its error state, the save form with the warning, with its box ticked, and after a submission without the box (#132), the address form on a bare `/save` and after a rejected and a flagged address (#97), the validation and refusal pages, the "Saved" confirmation, the "already saved" page, the list with the dialog open, and the delete confirmation page that a browser without JavaScript gets. Each scan first checks the scheme's ground color on `body`, so it scanned the palette it names. The other browser tests run in Playwright's default, which is light. The popup's axe test scans both schemes too, with `emulateMedia`.
  - axe runs in the page, so it cannot scan with `javaScriptEnabled: false`: its `evaluate` call never returns. The confirmation page is reached with `form.submit()` instead, which skips the dialog script's submit listener as a browser without JavaScript does.
  - The sign-in failed, sign-in link expired and account not allowed pages are reached only through Google's callback, which the browser tests cannot complete without the network. The integration tests cover them instead: `apps/worker/test/pages.test.ts` renders each one and checks its status, its headers and CSP, its stylesheets and header, and its markup with html-validate. They use the same layout and stylesheet as the scanned pages, and add no component of their own.
- The open delete dialog is centered, within 2 px, at 1280 × 900 and at 320 × 640.
- Continue is next to the address field at 1280 px wide, and under it at 320 px (#97). The no-scroll check alone would not catch a row that stopped wrapping, because the field would shrink instead.
- Since #97, `GET /save` shows the address form for a rejected address, so the browser tests reach the validation page with a link whose title is over its cap, and the refusal page with a tampered `POST`: the save form's hidden address is changed to one with an access token before it is submitted (`e2e/rejected-save-pages.ts`). The VoiceOver tests reach them the same way.
- With Playwright's `forcedColors: "active"`, only the current page's navigation link is outlined and underlined, the alert's icon keeps its "!" and its circle, and the save form's warning keeps its edge, and its rejected box its border and tick (#132). Chromium's accessibility tree, read over CDP, has no "!" text, because Playwright's aria snapshot leaves generated content out and could not catch it.
- On the signed-out page and the save form, `body` is set in Space Grotesk, both fonts load where they are used, and there is no `securitypolicyviolation` event, no console error, and no request off the Worker's origin.
- "Saved links" is the current page on the list only, and Sign out, without JavaScript, shows the signed-out page and ends the session. The check after it follows no redirect, so nothing goes to Google.
- The integration tests check that every HTML response's CSP is exactly the expected one, that each page links `tokens.css` and then `site.css` and has no inline style, that only a signed-in page's header has the navigation and Sign out, that the assets are served with their content types and `nosniff`, and that the build output contains them.
- The header comes before the content, so the two keyboard tests that pressed Tab once now expect the header's stops first: the wordmark on the signed-out page, and the wordmark, "Saved links" and Sign out on the save form.

The address form's tests (#97):

- **Integration tests** (`apps/worker/test/address-form.test.ts`) check the form on `/` and on a bare `/save`: its label, its `type`, `autocomplete` and `name`, the hint in `aria-describedby`, `novalidate`, no `required` and no placeholder, valid markup and no `style` attribute. They check the error state for each kind of invalid address, with the typed value kept and escaped, and for each kind of flagged address, with the reason and no trace of the link. A link from any source gets the same form, the validation page still lists several invalid fields when the address passes, and a signed-out request gets the same form without a redirect. `sign-in.test.ts` follows a signed-out bare `/save` through sign-in to the form, with `/save` as the stored `return_to`.
- **Browser tests** (`apps/worker/e2e/address-form.e2e.ts`) take the keyboard path: Tab past the header to the field, type or paste an address, and press Enter, which opens the confirmation form for it, filled in from the page (#105). They check the error state: the alert, `aria-invalid`, the accessible description (the error, then the hint), the kept value, that focus was not moved, and that correcting the value is enough. A flagged address leaves the field empty and the link out of the page.
- **VoiceOver** (`save-pages.voiceover.ts`): after Continue is pressed with VoiceOver, it should announce "Check the address", then read the heading and the error, and read the field as invalid, with the error as its description and the typed address. It was written without a local run, so its first CI run is its first check.

The address form's lookup (#105):

- **Pages are answered by Mock Service Worker (MSW),** in `apps/worker/test/page-fixture.ts`, which the integration, browser and VoiceOver tests start. Tests make no requests to the internet, so every address a test looks up is one of the fixture's pages. Their addresses are ordinary `https://` addresses under the reserved `.test` domain, such as `https://pages.test/`, so validation is the production validation, and no name is looked up.
- **Why it works.** `createTestHarness` makes every outbound fetch from the Worker with `globalThis.fetch` in the Node process that started the harness. It is the `outboundService` the harness sets, in `wrangler-dist/cli.js` of Wrangler 4.149.0, and it looks `globalThis.fetch` up on each call. MSW's `setupServer` patches that function, so it answers the Worker's requests in the test's own process. The harness's own requests, such as the integration tests' `server.fetch`, do not go through what MSW patches, so they are not affected. Playwright's request API does, when a browser test calls the Worker from Node, so the browser tests' fixture lets exactly the Worker's own origin through (`allow`, with MSW's `passthrough`).
- **No request goes unplanned.** MSW fails a request that matches no page (`onUnhandledFrame: "error"`). The Worker would only fall back to the address alone, so the fixture also records the address in `unplanned`, and each integration test and each browser test fails when that list is not empty. The browser tests' Worker always starts with the fixture, in `startHaystackWorker`, so the extension's tests are covered too. Their automatic `workerRequests` fixture checks the list after each test, so a failure points to the test that caused it, and the Worker's `close` throws on an unplanned request as a last check. A test shows that a request to `https://example.com/` is recorded and failed.
- **What this relies on, and what it cannot test.** It relies on the harness making the Worker's fetches from Node, which Wrangler does not document. If a Wrangler upgrade makes `workerd` fetch directly, MSW no longer sees the requests, every lookup fails, and the tests fail loudly rather than pass. Because Node makes the request, five things differ from production, and the tests do not rely on them:
  - Node follows redirects itself, so the Worker sees the final response under the first address, and neither redirect handling nor resolving the canonical link against the final address after a redirect is tested.
  - Node keeps reading a body after the Worker has cancelled it, so the cancellation of the rest of the body is not tested: removing `source.cancel()` from `readHead` fails no test. Whether the read stopped is tested by what the Worker took, and by when it answered (see below).
  - A request Node cannot make reaches the Worker as a 500 response rather than an error, so the `network` reason code is not tested.
  - The harness hands a request to the Worker as `http`, even when the test addressed it as `https`.
  - Node adds headers of its own, such as `accept-language`.
- **The fixture before MSW** was a local https server with a self-signed certificate, which the test process trusted through `tls.setDefaultCACertificates`. MSW replaced it (#105), because it needs no certificate, its addresses can be any `.test` host, and it fails a request no test planned for. A test-only `vars` value mapping fetched origins to a fixture, and a service binding to a fixture Worker through `bindingOverrides`, were rejected, because each would put a path that only tests use into production code.
- **Integration tests** (`apps/worker/test/save-address.test.ts`) check the happy path's 303 and every field in its `Location`, with the save form then prefilled from it. They check that values are decoded, matched case-insensitively, taken from the first tag with content, and cut to their caps; that the canonical URL is resolved against a `<base href>`, and kept when it is on another host; that a page in the charset its header names is read correctly; that the page is fetched without tracking parameters; and what the fixture saw: the User-Agent `Haystack (+<origin>)`, `accept: text/html`, and no cookie or authorization even when the user's request had both. A flagged canonical URL is dropped, and a page with long metadata gets a link within the budget, with `og_description` dropped first. Each failure redirects with the address alone and logs its code, and the logs never hold the address or the page's content: a page that never answers (after about 3 seconds), one that stops sending in its head, a network error, 404, 410, 500 and 503, JSON, plain text, XHTML and no content type, no metadata, only blank values, an empty body and a 204, a canonical URL over its cap or not an http(s) link, and a non-UTF-8 charset declared only in `<meta charset>` or `<meta http-equiv>`. An address that is already saved, whatever the case of its host, an address on the Worker's own host, and every rejected address are not fetched, which the fixture's requests show. A signed-out POST (401), a POST from another origin or without an Origin header (403), `GET /save` and `GET /save/address` fetch nothing. The JSON answers for the script are checked too: the save link with the page's fields and with the address alone, the headers, any `Accept` that names `application/json`, a rejected address with its error and whether the value may stay (never for a sensitive one, whose value never appears in the answer), 401 signed out, 403 for the Origin check, and that without JSON the same request still gets the 303 to the same link.
- **Reading stops early.** Three fixture pages never end, as `ReadableStream`s that the test stops, and a Worker that read to the end would never answer, or would answer at the time limit with the address alone. So getting the title back shows where the read stopped.
  - **At `</head>`, and at `<body>` when a page has no `</head>`:** after that point the page trickles on, one small piece every 50 ms, so a read that went on to the byte limit would take far longer than the time limit. The answer comes within half of it, and a meta tag right after that point, in the same chunk, is not read.
  - **At the byte limit:** a head of comments that never ends arrives as fast as the Worker reads it, so only the byte limit can stop the read before the time limit. Comments never reach a handler, so the element budget does not stop it first.
  - **At the element budget:** a head with 150 `og:site_name` tags before its title gives the first tag's value and no title. A head with 200 preload links before its title gives the title, because those links never reach a handler.
  - **Staying fast:** for each path that reaches a handler, a page of nothing but that tag up to the byte limit is looked up, at the fastest of three runs, less than 50 ms slower than a tiny page. The paths are `<meta name="description">`, `<head>`, `<title>x</title>`, an empty `<title>` and `<body>`. With the reader as it is, each measured 0 to 2 ms slower. Before the re-review's fix, `<head>` measured about 130 ms slower and `<title>` about 360 ms, and the meta tag about 90 ms without the budget, so the margin tells them apart with room for a loaded machine. `<body>` measured only about 10 ms slower before the fix, under the margin, so its case holds the path to the margin without showing that fix; removing the stop at `<body>` altogether makes it about 425 ms slower, which it does catch. Counting each chunk of a title's text is a second guard: no test fails without it, because a title's element is counted too. These are timing assertions, so if one proves flaky, the budget test above still shows the budget working.
- **Browser tests** (`apps/worker/e2e/address-form.e2e.ts` and `design.e2e.ts`) take the keyboard path from the saved links page and from a bare `/save` to a filled-in confirmation form. Each point of the form's design has its own named test, numbered as here:
  1. **Without JavaScript,** the form POSTs and follows the 303 to `/save`, unchanged, and a rejected address gets the server's page with the same error as with JavaScript.
  2. **With JavaScript,** the form goes through `fetch` to the same route, which answers JSON.
  3. **Continue is never disabled** (no `disabled`, no `aria-disabled`). Its label changes to "Processing…", and 100 ms later the processing message takes focus, whether Continue or Enter in the field was pressed, with the site's focus ring. A user who moved focus elsewhere first keeps it there (with Playwright's clock).
  4. **A second press with the same address is ignored,** and no second request is sent, whether it is Enter in the field, reached back from the focused message, or a click on Continue. Another test answers the lookup, keeps the browser on the page with a 204 for the save form, and presses Continue and Enter in the field again: no second lookup is sent. Playwright's locators wait for a navigation to finish, which a 204 never does, so that test presses keys through `page.keyboard` and checks which element has focus with `page.evaluate`.
  5. **A press with a changed address** aborts the request in flight and sends a new one, and the aborted request's late answer is ignored. One test clicks Continue; the other moves back from the focused message to the field, changes the address and presses Enter.
  6. **On success,** the browser goes to the same-origin `/save?…` link with its fields. A link on another origin, or to another path, is not followed.
  7. **On a rejected address,** the server's error markup is shown, and the field gets focus, with the error read first in its description, then the hint. A sensitive value is not put back in the field. The page takes the error page's title while the error is shown, and gets its own title back when a new request clears it. A corrected address clears the error, and a page that already shows the server's error gets it replaced, not doubled.
  8. **A timeout after 10 s** (with Playwright's clock), **a network error, 401 (signed out)** and any other answer each show their message, which takes focus, with a sign-in link for 401, and leave the form ready to try again.
  9. **The back-forward cache:** going back from `/save` brings back a form that's ready, and a page restored from the cache while processing is ready again, with its message gone and nothing focused (the test dispatches the `pageshow` event that the browser fires, because Playwright's Chromium does not show whether it used the cache).

  The form shown before sign-in loads no script, and even with the script added to its page, it asks for `GET /save`. In `design.e2e.ts`, each state the script leaves the form in (processing, a rejected address, a timeout, a network error and signed out), on both forms and with the message focused, passes axe in both color schemes and does not scroll horizontally at 320 px, and in forced colors the focused message keeps its 3 px outline. Every one of these tests fails when the behaviour it names is broken in the script or the route.
  - **Clocks are paused.** A test that moves time on installs Playwright's clock with `installPausedClock` (in `e2e/held-lookups.ts`), because `clock.install` alone leaves it running in real time, and a test could race the script's 100 ms.
  - **The review of #105** added tests that each fail without their fix: the region is not live once the script runs; the save link is followed without `URL.parse`; the error goes before the hint in the card and before the form on its own page; a value that must not stay is cleared only while the field still holds it; a failure message does not take focus from a user typing in the field; and a page the server rendered with an error gets its error-free title back after a failure.
  - **The lookups are held in the browser** by `e2e/held-lookups.ts`, with `page.route`, so a test can look at the processing state for as long as it needs, then let a request through, answer it or fail it. The script sends the form with `fetch`, so the page does not navigate while a request is held, and Playwright's locators work as usual. An earlier version cancelled each submission instead, because holding a navigation stops Playwright's locators until it finishes.
  - **The saved links page has two status regions** now, the list's and the address form's, so the delete tests find the list's with `e2e/list-status.ts`.

- **VoiceOver** (`apps/worker/e2e/address-form.voiceover.ts`): the test holds each lookup. After Continue is pressed with VoiceOver on an address without `https://`, the processing message takes focus and should be spoken. Once the request is let through, the server rejects the address, the script moves focus to the field, and VoiceOver should read the field with the error. Then a corrected address is answered with 401, and the signed-out message, which takes focus, should be spoken. The whole spoken log is searched first, as since 42b0867, then `waitForSpoken` (in `e2e/wait-for-spoken.ts`) looks at later phrases. It was written without a local run, so its first CI run is its first check. It assumes that VoiceOver's Space on the focused button submits the form, and that VoiceOver follows focus to the message and to the field, as it did in the investigation's runs. The page's clock is Playwright's, paused, and the test moves it on by the script's 100 ms itself, because on CI a press with Guidepup's `capture: true` waited for VoiceOver longer than the script's 10-second timeout, and the held lookup timed out before the test looked (run 39444075815). Presses now use `capture: "initial"`, and `waitForSpoken` listens after them. The same sequence, with an 11-second wait in real time, passed in plain headed WebKit and in Chromium. The server's error page is still read by VoiceOver in `save-pages.voiceover.ts`, which runs without JavaScript, because with JavaScript the error is shown in place and this test covers it.

The extension's browser tests (#70):

- They live in `apps/extension/e2e` and run in `vp run quality` with the other browser tests. They need Playwright's full Chromium (`channel: "chromium"`), which runs extensions headless. The headless shell has no `Extensions` protocol domain at all. Install it once with `vp -C apps/extension run e2e:install` (`playwright install --no-shell chromium`), and CI installs it before `quality`.
- The Worker exports its harness as `startHaystackWorker` (`@haystack/worker/e2e/fixtures`), and `@playwright/test` comes from the workspace catalog, because Playwright throws when one test run loads two copies of it. Each Playwright worker process starts the Worker, then builds the extension against the Worker's origin, because the origin is compiled in and the harness picks its port when it starts.
- Each test launches a persistent context with `--disable-extensions-except` and `--load-extension`, and clicks the toolbar button with `Extensions.triggerAction` (see Q4 for the details).
- The extraction function's tests (#72) use a plain browser context, not these fixtures, because they never load the extension. They keep the same rule: they serve the fixture page through a route, abort any other request, and fail if there was one.
- Playwright does not see the first request of a tab that the extension opens with `tabs.create`. Its routes don't intercept that request, it fires no `request` event, and extra HTTP headers are not added to it. Two things follow:
  - `signIn` adds the session as a real cookie with `addCookies`, with a `domain` and `path`, so the browser sends it on that first request.
  - The browser resolves no host but the Worker's (`--host-resolver-rules`), so that request cannot reach the network whatever it is. Every request Playwright does see must go to the Worker, the extension or a fixture page, and anything else is aborted and fails the test. Fixture pages are https documents served by a route, because Haystack saves only https pages.
- The popup tests rely on `PW_CHROMIUM_ATTACH_TO_OTHER=1`, an environment variable that Playwright does not document. The extension's `playwright.config.ts` sets it.
  - What it does: Playwright's Chromium code (`crBrowser`) attaches to targets of the type `other` and treats them as pages. Without it, Playwright detaches from them.
  - Why the tests need it: the popup's target starts with the type `other`, so without the variable the tests can click the toolbar button but never see the popup. They could not check the refusal message or run axe on it.
  - If a Playwright upgrade removes it, the popup tests fail at once, waiting for the popup, rather than pass without checking anything.
  - Schalk decided to keep it (#70), and to ask Playwright to support it upstream. The request is a comment on [microsoft/playwright#40554](https://github.com/microsoft/playwright/issues/40554#issuecomment-6226217824), which asks for the variable to be documented or replaced by a supported option.
- axe runs in the popup in legacy mode (`setLegacyMode`), because by default it opens a new page to gather its results, and Chromium closes a popup when another page takes focus. The popup has no frames, which are the only thing legacy mode skips.

**Firefox manual check** (#71). Firefox is covered by a manual check and by `web-ext lint`, not by Playwright. Playwright cannot load extensions in Firefox, and Selenium with geckodriver still could not click the toolbar button. The lint runs in `vp run quality` (see "Built in #71" under "Extension"), so a manifest that addons.mozilla.org would reject fails CI. The manual check covers what the lint cannot: the popup running in Firefox. Run it before Phase 5 is called done, and after any change to the popup, the manifest or the Firefox build. It needs Firefox 140 or later, the version the manifest requires.

1. Install dependencies with `vp install`. The first time, create the local database with `vp -C apps/worker run db:migrate:local`.
2. In a terminal, start the Worker with `vp -C apps/worker run dev`, and wait until it listens on `http://localhost:5173`. 1Password may ask you to approve access to the secrets.
3. In a second terminal, build both targets against the local Worker with `vp -C apps/extension run build:local`.
4. Open Firefox from the Dock or Finder, not from a terminal (the note after these steps says why). Go to `about:debugging#/runtime/this-firefox`, choose "Load Temporary Add-on…", and pick `apps/extension/dist/firefox/manifest.json`. Firefox keeps a temporary add-on until it quits, so load it again after a restart, and after each new build.
5. Open `http://localhost:5173`. Haystack redirects to Google sign-in. Sign in with an address that is in `ALLOWED_EMAILS`. The session lasts 30 days and stays in that Firefox profile, so later checks need no sign-in.
6. Find the "Save to Haystack" button in the toolbar, or in the Extensions menu (the puzzle piece). Pin it to the toolbar if you like. Note any prompt Firefox shows about the add-on or about data collection, and what it says.
7. **Check A, an https page.** Open `https://example.com/` in a new tab and click the button. Expect a new tab with Haystack's save form, which shows `https://example.com/` as read-only text after "Address:", and whose Title field holds "Example Domain". The popup closes by itself.
8. **Check B, a sensitive URL.** Open `https://example.com/?access_token=manual-check` and click the button. Expect the popup to stay open with the heading "This page can't be saved" and an explanation about access tokens, and no new tab.
9. **Check C, a page that cannot be scripted.** Open `https://addons.mozilla.org/en-US/firefox/` and click the button. Firefox does not let extensions run scripts on that site, so the popup falls back to the tab's own title. Expect a new tab with the save form, holding that page's address and its title. The popup closes by itself.
10. **Check D, the popup closes.** In checks A and C, the popup is gone once the save form has opened. In check B it stays, because it is showing the reason.
11. Record the Firefox version (Firefox menu, About Firefox) and one line per check, A to D, saying passed or what happened instead. Put them in the pull request, and in the Q4 result line.

Stop the Worker with Ctrl+C.

**Why not `web-ext run` from a terminal.** On macOS, `vp -C apps/extension run run:firefox` failed for Schalk in two ways on 2026-10-17. macOS protects one app's data against other apps, and it refused the terminal access to `~/Library/Application Support/Firefox`, even though the files belong to the user. The system log recorded an error for that protection (`kTCCServiceSystemPolicyAppDataDetailed`), and macOS showed no prompt to allow it. A program started from a terminal usually counts as part of that terminal, so the Firefox that web-ext starts is most likely refused in the same way, which would explain the second failure. That part was not proven.

- With a kept profile (`--keep-profile-changes`), web-ext reads Firefox's `profiles.ini` to make sure the profile is not the user's everyday one, and fails with `EPERM`. That is why the script no longer keeps a profile.
- With a fresh profile, Firefox itself shows "Profile Missing", and web-ext then fails with `ECONNREFUSED`, because Firefox has quit.

The script should work where nothing blocks that access, for example with Full Disk Access granted to the terminal on macOS, which is broader than this needs. It starts with a fresh profile, so sign in at the start of each run. If `web-ext` cannot find Firefox, or you want another edition, pass `--firefox` after the script name, with a path to the binary or one of the aliases `beta`, `nightly` and `deved` (Developer Edition). Stop it with Ctrl+C.

**Screen reader tests** use Guidepup (`@guidepup/guidepup` and `@guidepup/playwright`), which drives VoiceOver on macOS and records every phrase it speaks. The browser tests check the DOM and the accessibility tree, but not what a screen reader says. The first trial runs showed the difference: VoiceOver read a focused dialog's name and heading, but not its description (#56). Set up in #55:

- The tests live in `apps/worker/e2e` as `*.voiceover.ts`, with their own config (`playwright.voiceover.config.ts`), and run with `vp -C apps/worker run test:voiceover`. `e2e/voiceover-fixtures.ts` combines the browser test fixtures with Guidepup's `voiceOver` fixture through Playwright's `mergeTests`, so each test gets the local Worker, `signIn`, and a running VoiceOver.
- They run one at a time in headed WebKit, Playwright's closest browser to Safari, because there is only one VoiceOver and it cannot read a headless browser. They have one retry, because VoiceOver sometimes fails to start ("Unable to activate application"). That happened to the first test in two of six trial runs, and each time the next test in the same job started VoiceOver normally.
- They are not part of `vp run quality`, because they need macOS with VoiceOver set up for automation, and VoiceOver takes over the machine while they run.
- Checks match the parts of a phrase that matter (for example "heading level 1" and "Saved links"), not whole phrases or a snapshot of the log, because VoiceOver's wording changes between macOS releases.
- Guidepup records one joined entry per command, taken from VoiceOver's last phrase. With `capture: true`, Guidepup keeps looking after a new phrase, waiting about as long as that phrase takes to say between looks, so a long hint can swallow a short phrase that follows it. That is why the status message at first looked as if VoiceOver had never spoken it, when it had (#56). A test that waits for a short phrase uses `waitForSpoken` in `delete-dialog.voiceover.ts`. It uses `capture: "initial"` (Guidepup's default), which looks once and stops, and presses Shift (which does nothing in VoiceOver or on the page) repeatedly, so that Guidepup looks again after each press, until a phrase matches. For anything that VoiceOver moves to, `voiceOver.itemText()` (the text of the item under its cursor) is more reliable than the spoken log.
- The `@guidepup/setup` tool is a dev dependency too, run by the `voiceover:setup` and `voiceover:install` scripts, so the lockfile, the release-age rules and Dependabot cover it. (The Guidepup GitHub Action is archived.) Its optional screen recording depends on `ffmpeg-static`, whose install script downloads an ffmpeg binary without checking it, so `pnpm-workspace.yaml` denies that script, and the setup is never recorded. `voiceover:install` picks the VoiceOver assets that the installed `@guidepup/guidepup` version lists for each macOS version, so the two packages are updated together.
- `@guidepup/playwright` and `@guidepup/guidepup` are raised together. `@guidepup/playwright` declares the lowest `@guidepup/guidepup` it works with as a peer dependency, and pnpm warns about an unmet peer dependency but installs anyway, so the Quality job passes and only the VoiceOver job notices. That happened in Dependabot's #178: `@guidepup/playwright` 0.20.0 needs `@guidepup/guidepup` 0.36.1 or later, the project had 0.35.0, and every VoiceOver test failed with "macOSActivateId is not a function". Raise both packages in the same change. Dependabot proposes them together, in its `guidepup` group (see "Dependency updates", #185).
- CI: the VoiceOver workflow runs on GitHub's `macos-26` runner (free, because the repository is public) for pull requests and pushes to `main` that change anything under `apps/` or `packages/`, or the dependencies, and on manual dispatch. Since 2026-10-23 it skips Dependabot's pull requests, which waited a long time for a macOS runner, except updates to GitHub Actions and to the Guidepup and Playwright packages, which change the tests' own tooling. The push to `main` after a merge still runs it, so a dependency that breaks a VoiceOver test is caught there. The label fixes the macOS major version, but the image still gets 26.x point releases, which can change what VoiceOver says. The job runs `voiceover:setup --ci`, `voiceover:install`, then the tests, and always uploads the Playwright traces and videos, which Playwright keeps only for failed attempts. It is advisory, not a required check.
- Locally the tests are optional. Set up the Mac once:
  1. Run `vp -C apps/worker run voiceover:setup --macos-ignore-tcc-db`. The flag skips the update to the macOS permissions database, which needs System Integrity Protection disabled. Do not disable it: the setup asks for the same permissions by hand instead, as Guidepup's "Manual VoiceOver setup" guide describes. It opens VoiceOver Utility and waits up to five minutes until "Allow VoiceOver to be controlled with AppleScript" is ticked on the General tab.
  2. In System Settings, Privacy & Security, Accessibility, add the terminal or editor that runs the tests to "Allow the applications below to control your computer".
  3. Run `vp -C apps/worker run voiceover:install`, and again after each Guidepup update.

  While the tests run, VoiceOver speaks and takes the keyboard focus, so the Mac cannot be used for anything else.

- When VoiceOver behaves differently from what the markup and the specifications say, record it in the issue and report it to Apple. Do not work around it in silence.

**Evaluations** live in `evals/` and are separate from the test suite. They run only when explicitly invoked with a live key. The labeled dataset is a set of queries, each with the bookmark IDs that should and should not match. It starts with roughly 30 queries written by Schalk against his own bookmarks.

## Milestones and phases

Each phase lists its tests first. A phase is done when its tests pass and its acceptance criterion holds. Each milestone ends with a production deploy.

### Milestone 1: Capture

Goal: save bookmarks from the extension into production, so the collection starts growing. There is no enrichment and no search in this milestone.

#### Phase 0: Scaffold

Q10 is resolved. The Vite starter files and their `dev`, `build`, and `preview` scripts were removed in #2. Phase 0 is split into issues #5 to #8. #5 sets up the workspace layout, TypeScript, Wrangler, the Cloudflare Vite plugin, and the test harness described in Q10 (a). Playwright is set up in #11, before Phase 3. The remaining steps set up Drizzle with the D1 driver and drizzle-kit, varlock with 1Password, `varlock-wrangler`, the `secrets.required` declaration, and the D1 database with its initial migration generated from the Drizzle schema. Milestone 1 needs only the `users`, `sessions`, `auth_flows`, and `bookmarks` tables. The enrichment and search tables arrive in their own milestones through reviewed migrations.

Tests: a smoke test that the Worker responds through the test harness, a test that the migrated database contains the expected tables after `applyD1Migrations`, a test that the static assets directory contains no HTML files (run against the build output, not the source tree), and `vp check` and the `quality` script passing on the scaffold. The Q10 (a) experiments are recorded in Q10 (a). The Q13 experiment is recorded in Q13. Neither is kept as a test.
Acceptance: `varlock-wrangler dev` runs locally with `GOOGLE_CLIENT_SECRET` resolved from 1Password, no secret value appears in process arguments, and a migration generated by drizzle-kit applies cleanly with `wrangler d1 migrations apply`.

#### Phase 1: Capture payload schema

Write the Valibot schema for the capture payload, the URL normalization function, and `detectSensitiveUrl`, all shared from `packages/schemas`.

Tests: valid and invalid payloads, length caps, trimming, rejection of non-HTTP schemes for the URL and the canonical URL, a table of normalization cases (case, default ports, fragments, tracking parameters, parameter order, trailing slashes), and a table of sensitive URL cases: every signal in the list is detected with differing case, the credential parameters in the query string, the fragment, and a hash route's query, and the storage signals in the query string only (see "Refusing recognizable patterns"), embedded credentials are detected, and similar legitimate URLs (for example, documentation pages with `token` or `code` parameters) are accepted.
Acceptance: the Worker and the extension import the schema from one package.

#### Phase 2: Authentication

Implement Google sign-in for the site, sliding sessions, sign-out, the authentication middleware with the per-request allowlist check, and return targets. Q5 is resolved: use `oauth4webapi` for the Google flow, with hand-written sessions. The Google authorization and token endpoints, and Google's keys URL, are `vars` entries. Before writing this code, confirm the two checks listed in Q5 against the installed `oauth4webapi`. If it does not verify the ID token signature, verify it with `jose` against Google's published keys.

Tests: an invalid `state` or PKCE verifier is rejected, an ID token with the wrong audience or issuer or an expired one is rejected, an email not on the allowlist is rejected, a user is matched by `sub` even when the email changes, the session cookie carries the required attributes, a cross-origin POST is rejected, a return target is preserved through sign-in, an absolute or protocol-relative return target falls back to `/`, an expired session is rejected, a session in the second half of its life is extended, and a user removed from `ALLOWED_EMAILS` is rejected on their next request with an existing session, an `auth_flows` row cannot be used twice, an expired `auth_flows` row is rejected, and a return target longer than 4 KB survives sign-in. Stub Google's token and key endpoints with fixtures.
Acceptance: Schalk can sign in to the site with Google, and a non-allowlisted account cannot.

#### Phase 3: Save flow

Implement `GET /save` and `POST /save` as described under "Save flow." In this milestone, `POST /save` inserts the bookmark and does nothing else.

Tests: GET without a session redirects to sign-in and returns to the same prefilled URL afterward, GET never writes to the database, GET with invalid parameters shows a validation message rather than an error page, GET for an already-saved URL, including one that differs only by tracking parameters or a fragment, links to the existing bookmark, the title and description are prefilled by the fallback rules, edited values are stored as `title` and `description` while the raw captured values are stored unchanged, the form shows and `POST /save` stores the URL with tracking parameters removed (including when the hidden input was tampered with to add them back), the sensitive URL check runs on the URL before stripping, POST without a session or with a cross-origin `Origin` is rejected, a `javascript:` or `data:` URL is rejected, the same URL can be saved independently by two users, the user ID comes from the session and never from the form, application code does not log `/save` query strings, a sensitive URL is refused on GET and on POST without echoing the URL, the form shows the sensitive URL notice, two concurrent submissions of the same URL produce one bookmark and an "already saved" result rather than an error, a title containing markup is rendered as text with the Content Security Policy header present, and the rendered save form passes html-validate (issue #3).
Acceptance: opening a hand-built `/save` URL while signed in, then confirming, creates a bookmark under the right user with the confirmed title and description. The form works with JavaScript disabled.

#### Phase 4: Recent saves list

Build a server-rendered page at `/` listing the signed-in user's bookmarks, newest first, with a delete control on each item (see "Deleting bookmarks"). This makes deletion available from Milestone 1, so a link saved by mistake can be removed straight away. Each item shows the confirmed title as a link to the resource, the URL, and the date saved. It uses a heading and a semantic list, and works without JavaScript. The search page replaces it at `/` in Milestone 3, and the list may move to its own route then.

Tests: the sensitive URL notice is present, only the signed-in user's bookmarks appear, the order is newest first, an empty collection shows a helpful empty state, and the page passes an axe scan. Deletion: the owner can delete a bookmark, deleting another user's bookmark returns 404 and changes nothing, a cross-origin delete is rejected, the URL can be saved again after deletion, the delete button's accessible name includes the title, the no-JavaScript confirmation page works, and with JavaScript the dialog confirms, focus moves to the next item, and the deletion is announced.
Acceptance: a bookmark saved through `/save` appears at the top of the list and can be deleted from it.

The list was implemented in #41:

- `GET /` shows the newest 50 bookmarks, newest first (`created_at`, then ID). Above 50, the page says it shows the 50 most recent. Search replaces the limit in Milestone 3.
- The "already saved" page links to the bookmark's anchor only when the list shows it. For an older bookmark, it says the bookmark is older than the 50 the list shows.
- Each item is `<li id="bookmark-<id>">`, which the "already saved" and "saved" pages link to. It has the title as an `<h2>` link, the address as text, the description if there is one (with its line breaks as `<br>`), and the date saved (`<time>`, formatted in UTC, because the server does not know the reader's time zone).
- `savedLink` renders an `href` only when the address passes `isHttpsUrl` (`@haystack/schemas`), and adds `rel="noreferrer"`. Any other stored address is shown as text.
- axe runs in the page, so the scans run with JavaScript enabled. The acceptance test runs with JavaScript disabled.

#### Phase 4.5: Screen reader tests with VoiceOver

Automate VoiceOver, so the tests check what a screen reader actually announces instead of what the markup suggests it should (see "Screen reader tests" under "Testing strategy"). It comes before Phase 5, because every later page adds announcements worth checking. It is numbered 4.5 so that existing issues keep their phase numbers.

Tests: the list page, where moving by heading reads "Saved links" and then each saved link's title (#55); the delete dialog, where opening it announces which link it deletes before any button, the first Tab reaches Cancel, a deletion is announced, and Escape returns to the Delete button (#56); and the save pages, where the invalid-link and refusal pages announce their title when the user enters the page and then the heading and the reason, and a rejected title makes the field read its error as its description when the VoiceOver cursor reaches it (#57, #83; see "Save flow" for what VoiceOver says and the markup it needed). The address form's error state is checked the same way (#97).
Acceptance: the VoiceOver tests pass in CI on the pinned macOS runner, and a failed run uploads a trace and a video of the failed test. The setup is not recorded (see "Screen reader tests" under "Testing strategy").

#### Phase 5: Extension

Build the popup capture, validation and truncation, and the `/save` URL builder. Q4 must be resolved first.

Tests: browser tests that run the extraction function in Chromium against fixture documents, unit tests for truncation to the shared caps without splitting a surrogate pair, for the encoded-length budget that drops optional fields in order, for a URL whose serialized form exceeds the cap being refused (not truncated), for the fallback on pages that cannot be scripted, and for a sensitive URL stopping the flow before any tab is opened. A Playwright test loads the extension, opens a fixture page, clicks the action, and asserts that the prefilled `/save` form opens.
Acceptance: in Chrome (automated) and Firefox (manual check), the save form opens prefilled from the current page.

#### Phase 6: Capture deploy

Deploy to a `workers.dev` subdomain, following "Deploys" under "Secrets and configuration": confirm that a deploy fails while the secret is missing, set the secret in Cloudflare, take the export and apply the migrations remotely, run `vp -C apps/worker run deploy`, and add the production redirect URI to the shared Google OAuth client. Then build the extension against the production origin, and run the Playwright suite against production. Read "Backups and restore" under "Data model" before the first real bookmark is saved.

Daily use in Firefox needs a signed extension, because release builds of Firefox install unsigned extensions only temporarily. Sign it through addons.mozilla.org as an unlisted (self-distributed) add-on. An unlisted add-on is not published, so Q6 does not block it, but confirm that the name can change later. When installing the signed add-on, record what Firefox asks about data collection, and check that it names the two kinds the manifest declares (browsing activity and website content). The manual check in #71 loaded the add-on temporarily, and no prompt appeared. In Chrome, loading the unpacked extension in developer mode is enough for v1.

Acceptance: Schalk saves real bookmarks from the extension in daily use, and they appear in the recent saves list. Daily use also needs #99, the design of the Milestone 1 interface, which includes the reflow fix in #33, and #97, saving a link by typing or pasting its address. Without them, every page has the browser's default styles, and a link can be saved only from a browser with the extension.

### Milestone 2: Enrichment

Goal: every existing and future bookmark gets validated enrichment. The first backfill processes everything saved during Milestone 1.

_Where it stands (2026-10-28, #133)._ Everything Milestone 2 builds is built: the enrichment module (Phase 7), the sweep and its scheduled run (Phase 8), the backfill, which drained on 2026-10-28 (#130), the save form's check (#132), and the on-save path with the hourly schedule (#133). The on-save path and the hourly schedule were deployed on 2026-10-28, and Phase 9's production check passed the same day (see Phase 9), so Milestone 2 is complete.

#### Phase 7: Enrichment module

Add the `enrichments` and `enrichment_failures` tables and `sensitive_canonical_dropped` on `bookmarks` through a reviewed migration. The three `sensitivity_*` columns were already in Milestone 1's first migration (`0000_init.sql`), so this migration (`0001_enrichment.sql`, #120) adds only that one column to `bookmarks`. `describeUrlForJudgment`, the redaction that turns a URL into the only description Jev sees, is done (#121). It is a pure function and sends nothing, so it did not wait for Q7. Implement the Jev sensitivity check, and make the sensitivity gate the module's first step. Q7 must be resolved before anything sends bookmark data to a third party, which starts with Phase 7.3 sending the description to Jev. Write the Valibot schemas for the enrichment output and Jev responses. Implement the GitHub client, the Jev client with the `kind` question, the provider interface with the Anthropic and AI Gateway adapters, validation with a single retry, `match_text` derivation, and the atomic write of the enrichment row together with the removal of its failure record. A Jev failure after retries counts as an enrichment failure, like an LLM failure. The module takes one bookmark and returns an outcome. It knows nothing about how it was invoked.

Tests: the redaction never outputs a query value, fragment value, or random-looking path segment from the fixture URLs; the gate runs only when `sensitivity_noul` is empty; a bookmark above the threshold without an acknowledgment becomes Held and makes no GitHub, `kind`, or LLM call; an acknowledged bookmark above the threshold is enriched; a Held bookmark does not count as a failed attempt; and fixture-driven tests for every other branch, including a GitHub failure falling back to the captured payload, invalid LLM output being retried and then recorded as a failure, a Jev `429` being retried, a transient failure recording the error without incrementing `attempts`, a permanent failure incrementing `attempts`, a GitHub issue URL being enriched as the issue with the repository as context, a reserved GitHub route not being treated as a repository, an enrichment with an empty `use_cases` list passing validation, `match_text` for an empty `use_cases` list containing no placeholder text, `match_text` being deterministic, a second insert for the same bookmark and schema version doing nothing, and the enrichment row and the failure record change being written together or not at all.
Acceptance: calling the module on a fixture bookmark produces a valid enrichment row, and the bookmark's derived state is Current. _Built 2026-10-25 (#126): the module is `enrichBookmark` (see "Enrichment sweep"), and `apps/worker/test/enrich-bookmark.test.ts` has a test for every item in this list that goes through the module, and for the acceptance. The redaction's tests are #121's._

#### Phase 8: Enrichment sweep

Implement `enrichPending`, the `match_text` refresh, expired session cleanup, the `scheduled()` handler, the Cron Trigger, and the configuration for the batch size, the attempt limit, the frequency, and the enabled flag, as described under "Enrichment sweep." The enabled flag and the batch size are Flagship flags, the attempt limit is a `var`, and the frequency is the Cron Trigger's schedule in `wrangler.jsonc`, which changes through a reviewed deploy (#128). Q3 and Q13 must be resolved first.

Tests (the derived states for each case in the state table are already covered by `apps/worker/test/enrichment-state.test.ts`, from #120): the sweep runs when the harness's `scheduled()` triggers the handler, only Pending and Outdated bookmarks are selected, bookmarks at the attempt limit for the current version are skipped, raising `schema_version` resets the attempt count, one failing bookmark does not stop the batch, a second run with nothing outstanding makes no provider calls, raising `schema_version` makes previously enriched bookmarks eligible again while search can still use their older enrichment, raising `match_text_version` rewrites `match_text` without any provider call, expired sessions and `auth_flows` rows are deleted, the batch size is respected, a bookmark within its backoff window is skipped, a run stops early when its first calls all fail transiently, and the disabled flag stops the sweep but not the cleanup (#128).
Acceptance: `wrangler dev --test-scheduled` drains a local backlog of fixture bookmarks over several runs.

_`enrichPending` and the `match_text` refresh built 2026-10-25 (#127, see "Enrichment sweep")._ `apps/worker/test/enrich-pending.test.ts` has a test for each item in the list above that belongs to the sweep: the selection of Pending and Outdated bookmarks, the attempt limit, the backoff window, both schema version tests, the batch size, the concurrency, one failing bookmark, the early stop, the idle second run and the `match_text` refresh. The scheduled handler, the Cron Trigger, the configuration, the cleanups, the disabled flag and the acceptance are #128's.

_The rest built 2026-10-26 (#128, see "Enrichment sweep" and "Secrets and configuration")._ `apps/worker/test/scheduled.test.ts` covers the handler under the harness's `scheduled()`, the cleanups and the disabled flag, and `apps/worker/test/flags.test.ts` the configuration. The acceptance ran under `wrangler dev --test-scheduled` against the fixture server: 8 fixture bookmarks were enriched over 3 runs, of 3, 3 and 2, and the 4th selected none.

#### Phase 9: Backfill and on-save enrichment

Deploy with the sweep disabled, by creating `sweep-enabled` serving off first (see "Deploys"). Enable it for a single run at the starting batch size of three (Q3), and review those enrichments. While the flag is off, a Flagship outage runs the sweep with its defaults, enabled with a batch of 3 (see "Secrets and configuration"), so each run during the outage can enrich up to 3 more bookmarks that the review hasn't checked. Schalk accepted this on 2026-10-26 (see "Secrets and configuration"). Adjust the prompt or the schema if needed, raising `schema_version` if the output shape changes. Then enable the sweep and let it drain the backlog at Q3's starting size and schedule. Review any Held bookmarks on `/held` as they appear. Once the backlog is empty, add the Jev sensitivity check to `GET /save` and `POST /save` with the warning and required acknowledgment, record the dropped canonical URL's flag (the popup adds it to the save URL, the form carries it as a hidden input, and `POST /save` stores it in `sensitive_canonical_dropped`, see "Probabilistic check with Jev"; _built 2026-10-27, #131, ahead of the rest of this phase because it needs only the column_), add the on-save fast path to `POST /save` through `ctx.waitUntil`, and lower the sweep frequency to its steady-state setting. #133 measured `POST /save`'s CPU time with the on-save path: 17 ms and 20 ms for two real saves, far below the 30 seconds of Workers Paid (Workers Free allowed 10 ms until 2026-10-28). So the on-save path stays, and the sweep runs hourly. Had saves often gone over their CPU time limit, Schalk could have dropped the on-save path (Q3), and the sweep would then have stayed at every 5 minutes.

_Backlog drained 2026-10-28 (#130)._ The first run, on 2026-10-27, enriched 3 bookmarks, and after the account moved to Workers Paid, two runs on 2026-10-28 enriched the other 5, at the batch size of 3 throughout. Production has 8 bookmarks, 8 enrichments, no failures and none Held. The runs read their Flagship flags. Q3's "What #130 measured and decided" has the CPU and wall times, the `match_text` token counts and the decision to keep the batch size at 3. It also records the first review, of the summaries and the lists of all 8 enrichments, which found no invented claims, so the prompt and the schema are unchanged. The 10 ms that #133 once had to fit was Workers Free's limit, which no longer applies to saves (see the note in Q3).

Tests: `/held` lists only the signed-in user's Held and Failed bookmarks, retry clears the failure record for the current version and makes the bookmark eligible for the sweep, release sets the acknowledgment and makes the bookmark eligible for the sweep, delete removes the bookmark and its related rows, both actions reject cross-origin requests, the save form shows the warning and requires the checkbox above the threshold, `POST /save` re-runs the check and rejects an unacknowledged submission above the threshold, a Jev outage lets the save proceed with the noul left empty, the Jev state says the page declared a flagged canonical URL when the flag was recorded and never carries the canonical URL in any form, a bookmark saved without the flag is checked without it, a warning shown without a save logs no URL, the fast path does not delay the saved response, and a fast-path failure leaves the bookmark eligible for the sweep.
Acceptance: every bookmark in production is Current, Held and reviewed, or Failed with a recorded error, and a newly saved bookmark is usually enriched within seconds, or by the next sweep run, within about 5 minutes, if #133 dropped the on-save path (it didn't).

_The save form's check built 2026-10-27 (#132, see "On the save form" and "Calibration" under "Probabilistic check with Jev")._ `apps/worker/test/save-sensitivity.test.ts` has a test for each item in the list above that belongs to the save form: the warning and the checkbox above the threshold and neither below it, the check run again on `POST` and an unacknowledged submission rejected, a Jev outage saving with the noul left empty, the Jev state saying that the page declared a flagged canonical URL when the flag was recorded and never carrying the canonical URL in any form, a bookmark saved without the flag checked without it, a warning shown without a save logging no URL, and no Jev request for a signed-out request. The flag's tests cover each way it reaches the check: a save link that carries it, a canonical URL that `GET /save` or `POST /save` drops, and the form's hidden input. Each searches every request for the canonical URL's host, path, parameter name, token and encoded form, and checks the stored `sensitive_canonical_dropped`.

_The on-save path and the steady-state schedule built 2026-10-28 (#133, see "The on-save path" under "Enrichment sweep", and "Jev client")._ `POST /save` enriches each new bookmark inside `ctx.waitUntil` with `ON_SAVE_TIMING`, whose worst case fits the 30 seconds, and the Cron Trigger is hourly (`0 * * * *`). On Workers Paid the CPU time limit of a save is 30 seconds, so the on-save path stays, and the sweep doesn't stay at every 5 minutes. Until #199 is done, switching `sweep-enabled` off doesn't stop the on-save path, so it no longer stops all enrichment. `apps/worker/test/save-enrichment.test.ts` has the two tests in the list above that belong to the on-save path: the fast path doesn't delay the saved response, and a fast-path failure, returned or thrown, leaves the bookmark eligible for the sweep. Both changes took effect with the deploy on 2026-10-28, and these checks were left for after it, from the tail (see below): the CPU time of real saves with the on-save path, and whether any showed `exceededCpu`; how long real on-save enrichments took (`elapsedMs`), compared with `ON_SAVE_TIMING`; that a newly saved bookmark is usually Current within seconds; that the sweep runs at the start of each hour; and the acceptance above.

_Checked in production on 2026-10-28 ([#133's comment](https://github.com/schalkneethling/haystack/issues/133#issuecomment-6480589664))._ The deploy at about 20:25 UTC reported `schedule: 0 * * * *`. Schalk then saved two links through the address form, an ordinary page and a GitHub repository, and the on-save path enriched both. Their `POST /save` requests used 17 ms and 20 ms of CPU, and neither showed `exceededCpu`. `elapsedMs` was 5,390 ms and 7,375 ms, well inside `ON_SAVE_TIMING`'s 27 seconds, and each enrichment was stored 5.4 s and 7.4 s after its bookmark, so both new bookmarks were Current within seconds, which is what the acceptance asks. The tail's wall time for a save includes the work in `ctx.waitUntil`, so it doesn't show when the response arrived. The test in `save-enrichment.test.ts` that holds the LLM's answer is what shows that the response isn't delayed. Runs on the old `*/5 * * * *` schedule still fired at 20:30 and 20:35, and the first hourly run came at 21:00, and no run came at 20:40, so the new schedule took effect within about 15 minutes of the deploy. Production then had 10 bookmarks, 10 enrichments, no enrichment failures and none Held, so the acceptance above holds and Milestone 2 is complete.

### Milestone 3: Search

Goal: natural-language search over the enriched collection, with the logging needed to tune it.

#### Phase 10: Search page shell
